<header>Life Story.</header>
<meta>Catchword: as</meta>

# Life Story.

...us **GOD**, Amen. After this, we saw Him no more in this world.

Now, when Sunday morning arrived shortly after midnight, he called for his son **Tobias**, as may be read at the end of Letter C; among other things, he named some of his books that were known to him, requesting that they be reclaimed and called in. Long after this, he said: "One among you should go to Master **Schweinichen**" <note>Hans von Schweinichen (1552–1616) was a famed Silesian knight and biographer; Böhme likely refers here to a member of this noble family who acted as a patron.</note>; and thus, out of weakness, he spoke no further. What was meant by this, the **Noble Lords**, as his great patrons, will easily understand; they might perhaps meet the sorrowful, bereaved **widow** with a gift of grace <note>original: *gratial* — a financial grant or charitable gift</note> and occasionally come to her aid with something, or help advise her, as she has all her confidence in them regarding how she might spend the rest of her life. Just as the late **Jacob** had also said of her, that she would not survive him for long. He was thus—as the report contains—with a joyful countenance, gently and blessedly summoned from his thorns <note>A metaphor for his physical suffering and the "thorns" of worldly persecution.</note> into eternal rest by the **Father of Light** and passed away.

Since he died half an hour before the **City Gates** <note>The gates of Görlitz, which were locked at night for security.</note> were unlocked, without our **presence** <note>Kober refers to himself and other close friends who were not in the room at the exact moment of death.</note> but only in the presence of his family, I was soon summoned and went to them. We thanked **GOD** together that **GOD** had taken him to Himself and had granted him a gentle and quiet end for the comfort of us all. And because they had no one here who would take charge of his **body**, and they had asked him during his life before his end: "When you die, what should we do with you?" to which he answered: "In that matter, consult **Dr. Kober**." Upon this, I took charge of the matter, believing that since he had **received the sacrament** <note>original: *communiciret* — he had received Holy Communion, which legally and ecclesiastically cleared the way for a Christian burial.</note>, it would proceed quickly.

<meta>This page continues Dr. Tobias Kober’s account of the aftermath of Jacob Böhme’s death in Görlitz in November 1624. Following the previous page's description of his passing, the focus shifts to the struggle to secure a proper Christian burial against the fierce opposition of the local head pastor, Nicolaus Thomas.</meta>

# Jacob Böhme’s [End]

...which turned out quite contrary to expectations; for because there was still misery and mockery remaining—and since honor had not been shown to him while alive—it was intended that his body should also be committed to the earth with scorn.

Now, the <term>funeral sermon</term> <note>original: *Leich-predigt* — a formal sermon preached at a burial, which was often a status symbol in 17th-century Germany.</note> was arranged for the following Monday morning, alongside a written text from *Revelation 3:5* <note>"The one who conquers will be clothed thus in white garments, and I will never blot his name out of the book of life."</note> and the customary report of his life. A gold Ducat was also offered as an honorarium to the <term>Lead Pastor</term> <note>original: *Primario* — the senior clergyman of the city.</note> here (**Nicolaus Thomas**). However, as soon as he heard Böhme's name mentioned, he immediately pushed the request away with these words: **"Away with this! He would give him no funeral sermon; let whoever wanted to do it, do it. He had also vowed never to walk with him to the grave, for everyone knew with what <term>fanaticism</term> <note>original: *Schwermerey* — a derogatory term used by the orthodox church to describe mystical or radical religious views.</note> he had stained this city and other lands and people."** At this, we were indeed dismayed, but we comforted ourselves that GOD would surely help us provide for the corpse so that it might be placed in the earth.

Thereupon we immediately arranged for a <term>petition</term> <note>original: *Supplication* — a formal legal request to a higher authority.</note> to be drafted by Mr. **Michael Kurz** (as Document *Letter D* shows) to be delivered to the Mayor—since it was not a regular session day—which was to be done by the widow. Once the Mayor received it, he called the entire City Council together in the afternoon to <term>pass judgment</term> on it as a matter of great importance. After many conflicting <term>legal opinions</term>, and following the <term>sanction</term> of the lawyers—who argued that *it is human and pious for heretics to be granted a decent burial* <note>original: "Humanum & pium esse, hæreticos honesta sepultura affici"</note>—and following the testimony of **Master Elias Theodor** regarding Böhme's sufficient and reasonable <term>confession of faith</term>, they finally resolved to allow him a <term>funeral sermon</term> with the customary ceremonies. However, because the <term>Lead Pastor</term> had vowed against it, and even though he was ordered to do so, he still refused to perform it; nevertheless, the sermon was to be carried out <note>This implies the duty fell to the more sympathetic Elias Theodor.</note>.

<summary>Following Jacob Böhme's death, the head pastor of Görlitz, Nicolaus Thomas, refuses to bury him, calling him a fanatic; however, the City Council intervenes and legally mandates a proper Christian burial.</summary>

At this, we were glad again; and because the city was [already] locked <note>referring to the closing of the city gates at night</note>, I **proposed** <note>original: *proponiret*</note> that we should not bypass the **Lead Pastor** <note>original: *Primarium* — Nicolaus Thomas</note> again, but should send him the **Bible verse** <note>original: *Dictum* — the previously mentioned text from Revelation 3:5</note> that was requested for the sermon, along with the **Gold Ducat**. However, he **refused** <note>original: *renuiret*</note> again and spoke mockingly of the verse—which we would not wish upon him, lest he should otherwise look like **smut** <note>original: *Kohl-brandt* — a fungal disease that turns grain into black dust; a biting retort to the pastor's mockery of the "white garments" mentioned in the verse.</note>. Because of this, we received the payment and the verse back. Following this renewed dismay, we had another **petition** <note>original: *Supplication*</note> prepared by Mr. Hans Rothe to be handed to the sitting Council the next morning, being Tuesday; which was done, as documented in **Exhibit E**.

Meanwhile, I ordered the grave from the grave-digger and invited the people who were listed for the **funeral procession**. When the Honorable Council noticed our further pleading, they gave the answer: **Master Theodore** shall give the sermon, and the funeral shall be held in all things as decreed. And because the **preachers** <note>original: *Predicanten*</note> had only followed their own whims, they were forced to join the walk to the grave (even the "monk" <note>likely a derogatory reference to a village pastor</note> was requested from the village and brought in on a Council horse)—except for the Lead Pastor, who feigned illness and took medicine (namely, some **priest-fodder** <note>original: *Pfaffen-futter* — a satirical term for a rich, hearty meal.</note>).

Thereupon, with a great sensation among the people and in the **company** <note>original: *Comitatu*</note> of us, his faithful friends, as...

<meta>This page continues Dr. Tobias Kober’s account of Jacob Böhme’s burial in Görlitz. After the local clergy initially refused to provide a proper funeral, the City Council has intervened. This section describes the funeral procession and the reluctant sermon delivered by a local pastor.</meta>

# Jacob Böhme’s [Life]

for even other **shoemakers and tanners** <note>Böhme was a shoemaker by trade, and his fellow craftsmen showed solidarity despite the religious controversy.</note> who felt compassion for him, disregarding all the mockery, had the **Christian body** carried there by the youngest shoemakers and buried honorably in the earth. This was performed with the ringing of two bells and the attendance of the entire school—praise be to God. Although this was a heavy burden for the **widow and the children** because of the expense, we nevertheless arranged it this way for the sake of the gentlemen and his many good friends residing far and wide in the world. 

We would also have had other means at hand nearby with Mr. Ender at **Leutholzhayn** <note>modern Lutyń, Poland; Böhme’s friends had prepared a backup plan to bury him on a private estate if the city refused.</note>—as had already been decided—if we had received no favor or leave here. But we thank the dear God, who mediated the matter so far that the survivors can be at peace and need fear no dishonor before the world regarding their dear deceased father. Also, based on my report, the **birth certificates** <term>Gebuhrts-brieffe — official documents proving legitimate birth, which were essential for the sons to be accepted into trade guilds</term> for the sons shall be requested from Your Honorable Council; which will not be denied them in this **distress** <note>original: *Trüppel* — an archaic term for affliction or a difficult situation.</note>.

Concerning the **funeral sermon**: it was started with a strange and otherwise unusual introduction, in such a way that (not at all treating him as a Christian brother, as is otherwise customary) the pastor said he was now required to give the funeral sermon, but he would have rather walked 20 miles to please someone else than perform such a task. However, because this had been imposed upon him by Your Honorable Council, he must take it upon himself and perform it. The gentlemen will thus hear more of the sermon in **summary form** <note>original: *Summarien*</note>, as copied by Mr. Michael Kurtz (**Exhibit F**).

<summary>Dr. Kober describes how Böhme was buried with honors by his fellow craftsmen despite clerical opposition, and notes the officiating pastor's overt hostility during the funeral sermon.</summary>

# Biography

Now, when he <note>Referring to the officiating pastor, who remained hostile to Böhme even at his funeral.</note> was supposed to read the report at the end—concerning how and with what words he concluded his life—he left everything out (which is usually granted to everyone, no matter who they may be) regarding what was said in the conclusion after the **funeral sermon**; instead, he concluded with the **questions** <note>original: *Quæstionibus*</note> which he had raised against him **under confession** <note>original: *sub Confessione* — the private admission of sins to a priest</note>, some of which were attached **in the draft of the sermon** <note>original: *in Conceptu Concionis*</note>.

Thus, the **funeral ceremony** <term>Leich-Begängnuß — a formal burial service</term> has now been performed in the name of God, and the body has been committed to the earth. May Almighty God meanwhile grant the same rest in the earth, and on the Last Day a joyful **resurrection** from the dead to eternal life, most graciously to him and to us all, Amen.

Thus we have shown him the last service and helped to save and promote his honor and that of his family in the world, and we did not lack in counsel and deed. Especially since during the first six days after his arrival here, his wife was not at home, but had traveled to Dresden and **Bautzen** <note>A city in Saxony, south of Görlitz.</note> for the sake of her livelihood, we had no one to wait upon him. Therefore, we spoke to **Mr. Michael Kurzen**, who offered himself quite willingly and faithfully attended him day and night, lifting his body in and out and lifting and turning him, so that the blessed Jacob said to me: "Mr. Michael does me much good; if God helps me up a little, I will not leave him but will promote him wherever I know and can, as he is well worthy of it." For to my knowledge, no one here has followed him so faithfully in all admonitions and instruction, and made such **holy progress** <note>original: *profectus sacros* — spiritual or intellectual advancement</note> in a short time through

<meta>This page continues Dr. Tobias Kober’s account of the aftermath of Jacob Böhme’s death in 1624. # Jacob Böhme's

[Life and Defense]

Having attained divine granting, and freely without fear, without hypocrisy or the favor of men, he directed his <term>Sermons</term> <note>original: *Conciones*</note>, as well as his daily speeches, for the sake of the truth and love of **Christ**, in such a way that I believe he would sooner have walked through fire than lace the recognized truth with hypocrisy; indeed, his equal in steadfastness and sincerity has hardly ever come before me, which I may truly say of him. **God** will, I hope, let him become a special instrument; it would therefore be right that he might be promoted by the noble gentlemen as the opportunity arises. For this, I kindly ask as the first point.

Secondly, one has no reason to complain at all about the Hereditary Council <note>The local governing body of Görlitz</note>, for even though some were against him, the majority stood on his side and knew of no evil to attribute to him, especially since he had never been <term>formally summoned</term> <note>original: *vocirt*</note> by the Ministry of the Word <note>original: *Predig-Ambt* — the ecclesiastical office or clergy</note>, much less convicted of any wrongdoing.

Thus, thirdly, the blame is to be shifted to the <term>Clergy</term> <note>original: *Clerifey*</note>, who fled from [the truth] as if from poison before <term>Theriac</term> <note>original: *Thiriac* — a famous ancient medical compound used as an antidote to poison; the author implies the clergy saw Böhme's "cure" for the soul as a threat.</note>, and who would rather, I believe, have had the <term>burial</term> <note>original: *Sepeliri*</note> take place on the "Place of the Skull" <note>original: *Schedelstätte* — a reference to Golgotha, implying a burial place for criminals or outcasts</note> than in the churchyard, if we had not known other ways.

For this reason, this Christian brother had to suffer and endure not only much adversity, scorn, and mockery during his life because of his high gifts for the sake of **Christ**, but even his body has been held in such dishonor by them (regardless of the fact that all <term>preachers</term> <note>original: *Predicanten*</note> cry from the pulpit: *Of the dead, let nothing but good be said* <note>original: *De mortuis nil nisi bonum dici*</note>). May **God** only help so that no greater mockery may befall them and theirs as a reward; and so that <term>Master Elias Theodor</term> <note>original: *Mag. E. Theodorus* — a deacon in Görlitz who was more sympathetic toward Böhme than the head pastor, Gregor Richter.</note> may also speak <term>freely</term> <note>original: *liberè*</note> from the pulpit...

# Life’s Path.

...could <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of the funeral arrangements.</note> / he did not want to take any payment for the funeral sermon / but rather had it returned / which happened only out of fear and for the sake of the other priests, from whom he would have had to hear much <note>The officiating priest feared backlash from his more conservative colleagues for honoring Böhme.</note> / [claiming] he had only done what was customary / and had not made himself a participant in [Böhme's ideas] / but rather that he had been forced to it by the Council.

Thus, we have lost and sent on ahead a precious, enlightened, and dearly beloved man and father, highly taught by **GOD** / whom we should have honored and respected much more than was done. I mean us citizens of Görlitz, of whom we were not worthy; whom we mocked / whose name we did not like to mention / but rather publicly called a <term>fanatic</term> <note>original: *Schwärmer* — a derogatory term for religious radicals or "visionaries" common in the Reformation era</note>, an enthusiast, and a dreamer. Well, regardless of all that, he is gone. May **GOD** help us to recognize this / and that his words to us might not be fulfilled / which he often said to his friends and faithful brothers:
> Remember me / when I shall pass away / how **GOD** will deal with the city / much misfortune will befall it.

Now nothing more remains here / than that one should decorate his grave with one <term>proverb</term> <note>original: *Dicto* — a Latin term for a saying or biblical verse</note> or another. 
> Blessed is the man who endures temptation / James 1. 
> Blessed are you / when people revile you for my sake / Matthew 5. 

If the noble gentlemen wish to do something here / and have a decorative cross made for him with wide tablets / and a brass or gilded sun ☉ at the top / it is up to them / so that one might soon recognize his grave / which is nearly in the center of the churchyard or <term>God’s Acre</term> <note>original: *Gottes-Acker* — a traditional German term for a cemetery</note> / before all others. # Biography.

## Letter A. To the Distinguished Master and Doctor.

I shall not fail in my duty tomorrow morning, God willing <note>original Latin: *vol[ente] Deo*</note>, yet with the prior knowledge of the Lord Primary Pastor <note>original Latin: *Dn. Primario* — this refers to Gregor Richter, the head pastor of Görlitz and Böhme's primary ecclesiastical opponent</note> for reasons that I keep to myself. In the meantime, farewell and greetings from yours,

->Master Elias Theodor.<- <note>Elias Theodor was a pastor in Görlitz who attended to Böhme during his final illness.</note>

## Letter B.

The <term>questions</term> <note>original Latin: *Quæstiones*</note> which were raised with the late Master Jacob <note>referring to Jacob Böhme</note> by Master Elias Theodor, when he was to administer <term>communion</term> <note>original: *communiciren*</note> to him, were no more than these. 1. Whether he acknowledged himself to be a sinner? To which he answered "Yes." 2. There was a little book or teaching circulating; did he confess his belief in it? To which he also said "Yes," he confessed to it, and knew entirely that it would not be contrary to the true foundation of Christian teaching in the entire New Testament. 3. If God were to help him up again, would he also be willing to join the Christian community and assembly? To this he likewise assented as before. 4. Whether he intended to live and die upon the precious merit of the Son of God, our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ? When he affirmed this as well, the <term>preacher</term> <note>original: *Predicant*</note> gave him the Holy <term>Supper</term> <note>original: *Abendmahl*</note> and then spoke: Since he saw his current condition and weakness, such that he was not able to speak many words, he wished to take his leave again for now; but if the illness should last longer, he would visit him again; especially if he might happen to come to him alone.

# Jacob Böhme’s

->Letter C.<-

The <term>Life and Final Words</term> <note>original Latin: *Vita & verba Cygnea*—literally "Life and Swan-Song"</note> of our deceased, as we have recorded it in the briefest and simplest manner, and presented it to the Lord Preacher (to be read aloud after the funeral sermon).

Jacob Böhme, formerly a shoemaker here, was born of Christian parents into this world in the year recorded as 1575, in <note>Alt Seidenburg (Old Seidenberg) is a village near Görlitz, Böhme's longtime home.</note> Old Seidenberg. His father was Jacob Böhme, and his mother was Ursula; they were farmers there. By these parents of his, he was raised in a Christian manner and kept in school until he finally learned the shoemaking <term>trade</term> <note>original: *Handwerck*</note>, traveled thereafter, and finally became a master in the year recorded as 1594. In that same year, he also entered into the state of marriage with the then-virtuous maiden Catharina Kunschmann, daughter of Hans Kunschmann, formerly a <term>butcher</term> <note>original: *Fleisch-hauer*</note>. With her, he fathered four sons, one of whom is with God <note>i.e., deceased</note>, while three are still living; may God grant them His blessing. During their marriage, however, he conducted himself very peacefully with his wife, as well as with all his neighbors, as they are able to give good testimony of him. He also diligently adhered to God’s Word and to the Holy <term>Sacraments</term>. Regarding his illness, it lasted fourteen weeks, though he was not bedridden for that entire time. But when the illness gained the upper hand, and he noticed that his life would no longer continue, he reconciled himself with everyone,

-># Life’s Journey.<-
<meta>**** 6</meta>
<margin>Signature D2</margin>

He had also received the <term>Lord’s Supper</term> <note>Holy Communion</note> early on Friday; it was the 15th day of November. On the Sunday morning afterward, however, he called for his son *Tobias*, and asked if he also heard the beautiful music? When the son said no, he said that the door should be opened so that the singing could be heard better. Afterward, he asked what time the clock had struck. When they told him it had struck two, he said that it was not yet his time; in three hours would be his time. In the meantime, he spoke these words once:

> O you mighty **GOD of Sabaoth** <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *GOTT Zebaoth*—The Lord of Heavenly Hosts</note>, save me according to your will:

Afterward:

> O you crucified **LORD JESUS CHRIST**, have mercy on me and take me into your kingdom.

When six o'clock arrived, however, he took leave of his wife and sons, blessed them, and then said: "Now I am traveling into Paradise." He bid his son turn him over, and he sighed deeply, and thus passed away very gently and quietly from this world. His whole age reached 49 years, entering into his 50th. May God grant the body in the earth a blessed rest, etc.

# *Text of the Sermon.*
## *Revelation 3, verse 5.* <note>original Latin: *Apoc. 3. v. 5.*</note>

> He who overcomes shall be clothed in white garments, and I will not blot out his name from the Book of Life, etc.

It was requested that this passage be explained; but it seemed a ridiculous thing to the clergymen, and they did not want to do so, but only mocked and made sharp remarks about it.

# Jacob Böhme’s

## Document D <note>original Latin: *Lit. D.*</note>

### Petition presented to the Honorable Mayor regarding the corpse.

->Honorable, Respectable, and Most Wise Mr. Mayor;<- 

Since your Honor has been appointed by God as a patron, protector, and helper to all in general, and especially to grieving widows and orphans: I, now a grieving widow <note>Böhme's wife was Catharina Kuntzmann</note>, alongside my orphaned children, should rightly take refuge with you. I therefore most humbly request that your Honor would please accept my humble <term>petition</term> <note>original: *Supplication*—a formal written request to an authority</note> and plea. 

The matter is this: my dearly beloved husband and head of household, according to **GOD’S** will, passed away yesterday; now, I wished to bury him tomorrow, as is my duty according to Christian custom, and accordingly sent word in good time to the <term>Head Pastor</term> <note>original Latin: *Primarius*—referring to Gregor Richter, the chief clergyman of Görlitz and Böhme's long-time adversary</note> to arrange the funeral. 

However, the Head Pastor has refused—despite the fact that my husband did not act against the <term>Clergy</term> <note>original Latin: *Miniſterium*—the collective body of local ministers</note> at his end, any more than he ever did, as he received <term>Holy Communion</term> <note>original Latin: *communiciren laſſen*</note> this past Friday from Master <note>original Latin: *Mag.*—abbreviation for Magister</note> Elias Theodore—not only to give the funeral sermon, but even to accompany the body to the grave. 

What his objection in this matter might be, we do not understand. Therefore, as a grieving widow alongside my orphaned children, I take refuge with your Honor and humbly pray that you would, in your wisdom, help mediate in these matters, so that we may bring the body to the earth as soon as possible; for he was very swollen <note>likely due to dropsy or edema, which Böhme suffered from at the end of his life</note> and is not fit to lie unburied any longer; and to me as a...

## Petition to the Honorable and Hereditary Council.

Noble, Honorable, Respectable, Highly and Well-Wise, Highly and Well-Named, Most Favorable Commanding Lords: we cannot leave your Honors' High Wisdoms <note>A formal honorific for the city councilors</note> uninformed, out of our troubled and grieving hearts, how we—regarding the arrangement of our dear husband and father’s funeral, who now rests in God—once more and previously last evening approached the Reverend <term>Head Pastor</term> <note>original Latin: *Primarium*—referring to Gregor Richter</note>. He, however, was in total agreement with the Head Pastor; and thus both have refused that for which we approached them—according to the command and decree of your Noble High Wisdoms—and have raised all sorts of difficulties against it, and thus...

# Jacob Böhme’s

to this day, they have hindered and held us back so much in the continuation of our necessary Christian work.

Since, however, the condition of the corpse is such that it has swollen considerably from bloating, and it is feared hourly that it might burst, and thereby reach such a state that no one would be able to handle it; and therefore, it will tolerate no further delay or such lengthy formalities: we live in the completely comforting and obedient confidence toward your Noble High Wisdoms <note>A formal honorific for the city councilors</note> that, to prevent this misfortune in time, you will most graciously grant and allow us to carry out our Christian burial yet today and have him interred and laid in his little bed of rest. Regarding the funeral sermon—since it is the main point of difficulty, and because of envy and hatred it might be performed more to mock the blessed deceased than to honor him—we are willing to set aside. Nevertheless, we ask to proceed with the accompaniment of the entire school <note>Referring to the school choir, which traditionally sang during funeral processions</note> and other Christian ceremonies customary here, burial hymns, and songs at the usual time and hour. For this, we have obediently implored your Noble High Wisdoms for the sake of universal Christian love and the mercy of **Jesus Christ**, awaiting your swift and favorable decision. We shall repay such kindness to your Noble High Wisdoms with our faithful prayers to **God**.

->Your Noble High Wisdoms' obedient,<-
->N. N. Jacob Böhme’s surviving<-
->widow and heirs.<-

# Life Story.

->Document F.<- <note>original: "Lit. F."</note>

After the three preachers—**with the exception of the Master Pastor, who was ill** <note>original: "excepto Dno. Primario valetudinario"</note>—had to go along to the grave **whether they liked it or not** <note>original: "volentes nolentes"</note>, they turned away as soon as they came before the front door of the **Convent** <note>original: "Cœnobii"; likely the Franciscan monastery complex in Görlitz</note>. They did not follow into the church with the school choir as is customary. At this, everyone among the common folk pitied the good gentlemen, seeing that they had to go to the grave together with the body and were so incensed and enraged that the good gentlemen might have burst with anger. But **Master Elias Theodor** alone went into the church; and after the singing had been performed, he stepped forward and **cleared himself** <note>original: "purgiret"</note> before everyone with such a **preamble** <note>original: "Præambulo"</note>, stating that one should not by any means consider him to be the same kind of man as the deceased had been, simply because he was obliged to deliver the funeral sermon for him.

->**His actual words follow nearly exactly.**<- <note>original: "Sequuntur formalia ferme ipsius."</note>

Beloved friends <note>The original uses "x." as a placeholder for a standard congregation address</note>. Perhaps someone might wonder why I am stepping forward to preach in an unusual manner, since this is not my responsibility but that of the Master Pastor himself. Yet because this has been commanded and imposed upon me by your Honorable Council <note>original: "Erb-Rath"</note>, I have had to take it upon myself; though I would rather be twenty miles away, if I could avoid it.

Therefore, for God’s sake, I wish to have asked your Reverences and Honors that you do not think that I am perhaps also devoted to such error. For I testify publicly as before the Holy Trinity:
**1.** That I hold or teach nothing other than what I...

# Course of Life

...[they have] been disobedient to the Lord in the wilderness and at all times. But what Moses lamented then, we teachers and preachers must still lament today and say also of our listeners: **O that they were wise and understood**, etc. <note>Referencing Deuteronomy 32:29, a common text used to exhort a congregation to spiritual awareness.</note> For nowadays many are found who also reject their Refuge and Rock, and live in idle greed and pleasure, loving temporal and transitory things more than God and His holy Word; they are attached to the **lust of the eyes, lust of the flesh,** and a **prideful life** in deceit and cunning, etc. Therefore, we must not be silent and quiet today; rather, we must always lament: *Oh, that they were wise,* etc.

For just as we read in Exodus 28 that bells were made for Aaron’s robe so that he could not walk silently, etc. <note>Aaron was the first High Priest of Israel; the bells on his priestly garment ensured his movements were heard while he served in the Tabernacle.</note> So it also behooves us that we should never be silent, etc. Above all things, however, we must well consider the end and the outcome if we wish to find true prudence and wisdom; for this reason, Sirach says in Chapter 7:

-> **In all that you do, remember the end, and so you will never sin again.** <- <note>A famous verse from the Book of Sirach (or Ecclesiasticus) 7:36, emphasizing that the thought of one's own death is a deterrent to sin.</note>

Would to God they were wise and thought on this: For it is appointed for all men once to die, and after that the judgment, etc. <note>A reference to Hebrews 9:27.</note> Therefore David also prays in Psalm 39:

-> **Lord, teach me that there must be an end for me, and that my life has a goal, and I must depart from it.** <-

And in Psalm 90, Moses prays: *Oh Lord, teach me,* etc. For as an old teacher, Augustine, says: **Nothing so calls one back from sin as the frequent meditation on death.** <note>original Latin: "Nihil sic revocat à peccato quam frequens mortis Meditatio"; a classic theological principle used to encourage piety through the awareness of mortality.</note> So that we do not dwell any longer in the <term>Exordium</term> <note>original: "Exordio"; the formal introduction of a sermon designed to prepare the audience's mind</note>; but rather hear and learn something for our improvement from the passage read, we will take up this single <term>teaching point</term> <note>original: "Lehr-punct"; the specific theological or moral lesson the preacher intends to prove</note> to address:

### As
# I. What every pious Christian should remember and keep before their eyes throughout their whole life.

-> To this may God grant us [His grace], etc. <- <note>The abbreviation "2c." (etc.) in this context likely refers to a standard liturgical plea for divine assistance.</note>

1. **Common mortality** <note>original: "Communis mortalitas"</note>, of which the text says: **It is appointed for men once to die**; <note>Hebrews 9:27</note> This is to be understood **universally** <note>original: "universaliter"</note> of everyone in common / for no one is **excepted** <note>original: "except"</note> or excluded / whatever their status / honor / skill, etc. may be / yet they still cannot escape death / of this, alongside daily experience and the evidence of our own eyes, the Scripture bears witness: As when Sirach laments in Chapter 40: *It is a miserable, wretched thing,* etc. <note>Sirach 40:1 describes the "heavy yoke" and "hard labor" laid upon all people from the day they leave their mother's womb.</note> Isaiah 40: *All flesh is grass,* etc. For we are altogether sinners / and we all lack the glory / that we should have in God: But because of sin, death has come into the world, etc. Romans 5:6. Psalm 89: *Where is a man who lives and does not see death?* Because of this, temporal death is called here and there in the Scripture; as in Joshua 23 and 1 Kings 2: **The way of all the world.** <note>The OCR reads "Joh. 23," but this refers to Joshua 23:14, where the aged leader says he is going "the way of all the earth."</note> Thus, no one can avoid death / whether they be young, rich, etc. 
1. When a young person dies / people are saddened / because they believe / He could have lived for a good while longer to comfort and be of use to his family: But death does not spare / young people are carried out [to the grave] just as soon as old people, etc.
2. Everyone honors an old person, etc. and fears him: But death honors no one, etc. Even the people in the first world <note>Refers to the "Antediluvian" period—the era before the Great Flood.</note> / although they became very old / nevertheless had to die in the end / Adam / Seth / Me- <margin>Methusalah</margin> <note>The margin completes the name "Methusalah," who according to Genesis was the longest-lived human.</note>

[Me]thusalah, Noah, etc. 3. A rich man, even if he has much money and goods in his possession, cannot save <note>original: "salviren"</note> himself from death. Everything in this world—whether it be silver, gold, or money—lasts only a short time, etc.

4. Even if someone is in great honor and status because of high rank, wisdom, or skill <note>original: "Kunst," which in this era refers broadly to knowledge, art, or specialized skill</note>, etc. Therefore, Sirach says: **Today a king, tomorrow dead.** <note>Though the text cites Sirach 16, this famous aphorism is found in Sirach 10:10.</note> The wise heathens also recognized this when they said: **Death makes scepters equal to hoes**; <note>original: "Mors sceptra ligonibus æquat." This is a Latin proverb suggesting that death treats kings and common laborers as equals.</note> also, **Death knocks with an impartial foot at the hovels of the poor and the towers of kings.** <note>original: "Mors æquo pede pulsat pauperum tabernas Regumque Turres." This is a quote from the Roman poet Horace (Odes 1.4.13), illustrating that death is the great equalizer.</note> Therefore, death is rightly called **a most severe judge who neither fears nor respects anyone.** <note>original: "Judex severissimus qui non timet nec veretur quemquam."</note> Samson was an excellent and strong hero, etc., **but he did not prevail against death.** <note>original: "sed non prævaluit morti."</note> **Absalom was the most beautiful**; <note>original: "Absolon pulcherrimus." Absalom was the son of King David, famous for his physical beauty (2 Samuel 14:25).</note> Jezebel was beautiful and the daughter of a king; Solomon was **wise** <note>original: "Sapiens"</note>; Asahel <note>The OCR reads "Hafael," but this refers to Asahel, brother of Joab, known in the Bible for his incredible speed.</note> was **the swiftest** <note>original: "celerrimus"</note>: **yet they have died.** <note>original: "at mortui sunt."</note> And death has not only ruled over the godless, like Annas and Caiaphas <note>The high priests who presided over the trial of Jesus.</note>, at all times, but also over the saints of God; for they all possessed sinful flesh, over which death holds its dominion and reign, according to 1 Corinthians 15: *Flesh and blood cannot inherit the kingdom of God.*

**From this we conclude that mortality is common to all.** <note>original: "Ex hoc concludimus communem esse omnium mortalitatem."</note> O! If only we were now wise, etc.

2. **The inequality of the hour of death.** <note>original: "Horæ mortis inæqualitas." This point emphasizes that the timing of death is unpredictable and varies for everyone.</note> For no one knows when their end is appointed for them, or where death might come upon them. Paul teaches this in the passage read; also in Luke 7, we read of the widow's son at Nain, who died in his youth before his mother; **and other dissimilar examples of death, for nothing is so small and tiny that** <note>original: "& alia exempla Mortis dissimilia nihil enim tam parvum & exiguum quod." The sentence breaks off here as the page ends.</note>

# Jacob Böhme’s #

...cannot rob a man of life. *Examples of Death.* <note>original: "hominem vita spoliare non possit. Exempla Mortis." This concludes a sentence from the previous page regarding the inevitability of death regardless of one's strength or beauty.</note>
1. *Diogenes ate an octopus and died.* <note>original: "Diogenes Polypum comedit & moritur." A reference to a legendary account of the death of the Greek philosopher Diogenes the Cynic.</note>
2. Sophocles was struck dead by a collapsing vault. <note>original: "von einem Gewölb erschlagen." Sophocles was a famous Greek playwright; while there are many legends of his death, this text suggests he was killed by a falling architectural structure.</note>
3. *Acts 20:* the boy falls from the upper floor. <note>original: "fällt der Knabe vom Soller." This refers to the biblical story of Eutychus, who fell from a third-story window while listening to the Apostle Paul preach.</note>

Just as a person dies and departs from this life, so shall he be judged, and so shall he appear on the Last Day. Many, however, have departed through many various paths of death; now, if one knew when he were meant to die, he would surely prepare himself. But the time and hour are hidden from us and are very unequal among the children of men.

-> Oh, that we were wise and not so complacent, etc. <note>A reference to Deuteronomy 32:29: "If they were wise, they would understand this; they would discern their latter end."</note> <-

3. *The Proximity of the Appointed End:* <note>original: "Termini fatalis propinquitas." This is the third main point of the sermon.</note> This saying is drawn from the 14th chapter of *Sirach*, <note>The Book of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus. The text likely refers to the "decree from of old: You must die."</note> where it is said that a goal is set for man that he cannot overstep. Therefore, this goal is well worth considering for every pious Christian, as no one can hold it back. When one arrives at it, his race is run and his life takes an end. Like an arrow, when it is shot at a target: when it reaches the same, it falls. So too is the life of man, etc. Just as in a ship that sails upon the wild sea: whether the people sleep or wake, whether they walk or stand, sit or lie, they are always being brought closer to the harbor. So it is also with people, etc. Hence *Seneca* <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, a Roman Stoic philosopher who wrote extensively on the passage of time.</note> says: **while we grow, our life decreases.** <note>original: "dum Crescimus, vita nostra decrescit."</note>

-> Oh, that we were wise. <-

4. *The Difficulty of Dying,* <note>original: "Moriendi Difficultas." This is the fourth point.</note> that is, to die in these words. It is quickly spoken, that it is appointed for man to die once; but it goes hard for many, as we see. For in death, body and soul are separated and divided from one another; the senses, such as hearing, seeing, etc., cease; the soul travels on—

## Life’s Journey ##

...secure place, the body must decay and rot. No one can help when this process begins with a human being. An ancient teacher writes that the soul then sees all the works it has performed throughout its entire life when it must depart from the body. Even if some people comfort themselves for a while and delight in this time with worldly pleasure and joys, it is nonetheless all fleeting and must finally all be left behind. Therefore, <term>Zephaniah 1</term> <note>Zephaniah 1:18</note> says: Neither their silver nor their gold shall be able to deliver them, etc. **Gold deserts its possessor.** <note>original Latin: "Aurum deserit possessorem suum."</note> Man came into this world naked; naked he must also depart from here again, etc.

-> Oh! That we were now wise, etc. <-

5. **The Filth of Corruption.** <note>original Latin: "Corruptionis foeditas." This section discusses the physical degradation of the body after death.</note> When a person dies, he is no longer dear to anyone; he is carried away from his own people, just as in <term>Luke 7</term> the widow’s son was carried outside the city gate. For death makes us so ugly, gruesome, and abominable that no one wants to tolerate or have us near them anymore. But where has such misery come from, that we must decay so miserably with our bodies? Sin and Satan are the cause of this, according to the <term>Book of Wisdom, chapter 2</term> <note>Wisdom of Solomon 2:24: "Through envy of the devil came death into the world."</note>. When a person dies, the beautiful house of his body must be broken apart—which is indeed to be lamented—so cruelly does death tend to deal with us.

-> Oh! That we were now wise and would reflect while there is still time. <-

6. **The Singularity of Death.** <note>original Latin: "Mortis unitas." The theological concept that humans have only one life and one death to face judgment.</note> It is appointed for all men to die only once—not twice or three times—so that if one were to die poorly the first or second time, he might die better and more blessedly the third time. Consider this well: it does not happen with man as it does with trees, which, although they are often cut off at the root, yet again <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with the idea that trees can regrow while humans cannot return from death to try again.</note>

<summary>This page explores the physical and spiritual grimness of death, emphasizing that wealth cannot prevent decay and that death occurs only once, leaving no room for a "second chance" at a holy end.</summary>
<keywords>memento mori, Zephaniah, physical decay, Satan as cause of death, singularity of death, funeral sermon, Jacob Böhme</keywords>

...shoot up and grow anew, etc. To die badly once brings eternal ruin, etc.

7. **The Newness of the Dwelling Place.** <note>original Latin: *Habitaculi Novitas*</note> To wherever the soul goes, it must remain eternally, either in Heaven or in Hell. 1. Concerning the souls of the righteous, the Scripture says that they are in God's hand. <term>Wisdom 3</term> <note>Wisdom of Solomon 3:1: "But the souls of the righteous are in the hand of God."</note> <term>Revelation 14</term> <note>Revelation 14:13</note>. They are snatched away, etc. <term>Isaiah 29 and 56</term>, etc. <term>Luke 16</term>. *The soul of Lazarus is carried into the bosom of Abraham.* <note>original Latin: *Anima Lazari portatur in sinum Abrahae*</note> 2. *On the contrary, the souls of the ungodly* <note>original Latin: *Econtra Animae impiorum*</note> are carried and come into eternal unrest, as is to be seen in the case of the rich man in Luke 16. <term>Isaiah 66</term>, etc. Their worm does not die, their fire is not extinguished, etc. <note>Isaiah 66:24</note>

8. **The Severity of the Last Judgment.** <note>original Latin: *Judicii extremi Severitas*</note> The soul will be reunited with the body again; and then be placed before the judgment seat of Christ, where everything will be brought to judgment, whether it be good or evil: We will have to give an account of all our words and works, etc. Then things will go hard for many a person who did not take better care of themselves during this time. We must give an account:

-> 1. *concerning lost gifts.* <note>original Latin: *de Donis amissis*</note> <-
-> 2. *concerning neglected good deeds.* <note>original Latin: *de Bonis omissis*</note> <-
-> 3. *concerning committed evils.* <note>original Latin: *de Malis commissis*</note> <-

> Oh! That we were wise, etc.

These, then, are the points which a Christian person should constantly have before their eyes. When we then consider such things, why do we not flee from all vice? Why do we not hate all ungodliness, the <term>lust of the eyes</term> <note>A biblical term from 1 John 2:16 referring to materialistic or sensory cravings</note>, etc.? But so that we may do it and escape the coming severe judgment: may God help us all by His grace. Amen.

# Biography.

> (After finishing the sermon, he took in hand the announcement slip which we had sent to him; from it, he read the life of our blessed deceased until his last words, which he passed over at his own discretion, and afterwards brought forth the following.)

As for the person of the deceased, I cannot say that I knew him previously. However, when I was called to come to him and <term>administer Holy Communion</term> <note>original: *communiciren*</note> to him, I reported this to the <term>Senior Pastor</term> <note>original: *Primario*; the head of the local church district</note>. Although I received no <term>definitive answer</term> <note>original: *Responsionem Cathegoricam*</note> from him, I nonetheless did so after much deliberation. For I have always been willing to serve everyone as long as I have held this <term>office</term> <note>original: *Ambte*; his position as a clergyman</note>; indeed, in one morning I once <term>administered communion</term> to eleven people, and I have been willing to serve everyone who desired me, by day or by night. When I came to him, I first sought information by asking him several questions; he then answered quite correctly and made his <term>confession of faith</term> <note>original: *Bekäntnuß*</note>, with which his heart seemed to be in good and proper agreement.

(The questions raised above are noted under Letter B. Now he added: He had asked whether he would renounce his error and in the future hold to the preaching and the Holy <term>Sacraments</term>? To this he said yes, if God would help him up. Thereupon—the <term>preacher</term> continues—I <term>administered communion</term> to him; and afterward exhorted him that in the future he should not wait for <term>raptures</term> <note>original: *Raptus*; a state of being seized by a vision</note> and <term>ecstasies</term> <note>original: *Entzückung*; a trance-like state of spiritual joy</note>; but should simply hold to the Word of God, etc., for such imaginings might be deceptive, and the Devil could easily weave himself into them and disguise himself as an angel of light to deceive man; also...)

<meta>This page continues the account of Jacob Böhme’s final days and the controversial circumstances surrounding his funeral. # Jacob Böhme’s
# In

He said that he had exhorted him to read not only the New Testament but also the Old Testament, etc. Just as the lawyers say, <note>original: *in dubiis semper benigniora sequuntur*</note> **in doubtful matters, the more favorable interpretation is always followed**: so we also must speak the best in doubtful cases; and perhaps our deceased turned to <term>repentance</term> <note>original: *Busse*</note> and was converted at his end; although we have no greater example of a late yet earnest repentance than that of the <term>thief on the cross</term> <note>original: *Schächer am †*; the criminal crucified alongside Jesus who sought forgiveness at the last moment</note>. So I would have you exhorted, my beloved, that you take this from me in the best way, and be collectively and faithfully admonished to keep yourselves to the hearing of God’s Word, and not to despise holy absolution and the <term>sacraments</term>, but to use them.

You should also be exhorted that you should not judge, etc.

> May God grant the body a blessed rest in the earth, and a joyful resurrection. Amen.

Thus did this priest judge; so that we might well wish he had done it better or left it alone altogether; yet he also earned nothing but anger from those on his side, so that they scolded him not a little for not making it worse. One must let them go, for they are blind and leaders of the blind. May the Lord open their eyes, that they might yet see the bright light: They did not want to, and yet they had to; they also <term>refused</term> <note>original: *renuirt*</note> everything and would not take a single penny of the <term>blood-money</term> <note>original: *Blut-geldes*; likely a derogatory reference to the standard funeral fees, which the hostile clergy rejected to distance themselves from Böhme</note>; moreover, the <term>Senior Pastor</term> <note>original: *Primarius*</note> addressed the <term>messenger</term> <note>original: *Läuffer*</note> and the <term>funeral inviter</term> <note>original: *Grabebitter*; a traditional role for one who formally invited the community to a burial</note>, regarding how he was involving himself with such people. May the Lord not hold all this against them: But I wish for myself that my end may be like his end, Amen.

->Michael Kurtz,<-

# Life Course
<note>original German: *Lebens-Lauff*</note>

## *On the Death of the Author.*
<note>original Latin: *In Obitum Authoris*</note>

**M**uses of Nyssa <note>original: *Nissiades Musæ*; a classical reference to the nymphs who raised Dionysus, often invoked as Muses in Baroque poetry</note>, if your hearts are not made of iron, hasten here quickly, and redouble with me the sad complaint which strikes the stars with pitiful lamentation. Who will stop the tears for the mournful, and block the springs of sorrow? Who will turn all things from darkness toward the better? Behold, fierce Autumn, which strips the fields of grass and the groves of their leaves and the forests of their beauty, brings fates far too sad for the wretched, and stirs up laments while the urn snatches you away, such a man, **Jacob**, **O Böhme**, the glory recognized by the few who are better. Therefore I shall lay my limbs beneath the sad Cypress <note>a tree traditionally associated with mourning and cemeteries</note>, singing sad songs for you with a complaining voice, **O Father**, and the greatest part of our life, **Böhme**. 

I will not boast of an ancient lineage or a more noble family tree, which the World loves and descendants brag of; for a poor mother brought him into the light of day, born of a farmer father. Why is this a wonder? For this is not the "noble" lineage that pleases the world; piety is the entire lineage and family tree of the good: this accompanied him as a boy, a youth, and even as an old man; through this you shall live, famous among those who remember you after your funeral. 

Who taught the boy? A learned Schoolmaster? What School made you such as you are? Was it the cultured <term>Lyceums</term> <note>original: *Lycæa*; referring to elite secondary schools or universities</note>? Was it Plato the Philosopher, or any more recent sage? You were stuck in the smallest tasks, and in the lowly dust, though despised, you handled the work of your hands <note>a reference to Böhme's trade as a shoemaker</note>: but the fact that your holy writings are now read everywhere across the wide world was brought about by Him alone, by whom all pride is scorned, and who loves the humble, preparing praises from the mouths of children and the pleasing gifts of tongues in the lowest places. Three sons remain for you on earth from one wife, but I doubt further if your male offspring is triple...

->*****<-

# Jacob Böhme’s

Whether antiquity shall extend his life through his writings. <note>original: *An scriptis fuerit vitam extensura vetustas*; a poetic way of asking if Böhme's fame will endure through the ages because of his books.</note>
What **GOD** and powerful Nature are, what the Earth and the Heavens,
What an Angel and Satan are, what man is, and what the enclosures of the Abyss <term>Barathrum</term> <note>a Latin term for a deep pit or the underworld, used here to describe the depths of Hell or the primordial void</note> are,
And by what way the wretched might reach Christ, and which path must be trodden,
You taught all these things by divine warning and guidance.
Satan opposed you in vain: because the right hand of the Almighty
Covered you throughout your whole life like the pupil of the eye. <note>A biblical allusion to Psalm 17:8, emphasizing Böhme as being under special divine protection.</note>
Now that your course is finished, you ascend to the stars, leaving
Heavy cares and the triple bond <note>The "triple bond" likely refers to Böhme's theological concept of the three-fold life or the constraints of the material, astral, and elemental worlds.</note> behind on
Earth, repeating a sad farewell: we, in our sorrow, follow
Your funeral with weeping and warm eyes, and we wish thereafter
That we too might be permitted to break the dire arrows of death
Just as soon, in Christ, who is our life.
Therefore, farewell for Eternity, and enjoy eternal rest:
The wild crowd of evils will not vex you, but it vexes us;
Once all these are conquered, we shall follow you with a joyful mind.
At last, in the new spring, when before the Judge’s tribunal
Whatever the devouring earth and the somber underworld <note>original: *orcus*; a classical name for the realm of the dead.</note> have hidden
Shall be presented, we too shall be joined together with the
Holy assemblies; so that we may sing the praises of **JEHOVAH** <note>original: *JOVÆ*; a Latinized form of the Hebrew name for God, often used in scholarly Latin poetry.</note> without end.

<meta>catchword: 7. Whether</meta>

Did He [Christ] take [human nature] in the fullness of time <note>A biblical phrase from Galatians 4:4 referring to the moment of Christ's birth.</note> from the **Virgin** <term>virgin</term> Mary by the **work of the Holy Spirit** <term>Operatione Spiritus sancti</term> / in unity of person, unmixed and undivided / and did He not bring His flesh from heaven / much less lay it aside after His resurrection and ascension: but is and remains God and man for all eternity?

->Answer: Yes.<-

5. That a Christian person should lead a holy and blameless life and conduct according to **God’s** command: (As far as is possible **in this corrupt nature** <term>in hac corrupta natura</term> <note>Refers to the human condition after the Fall, which is viewed as inherently sinful.</note>) But can earn nothing thereby with **God** / according to the saying of Christ / "when you have done everything" **etc.** <term>zc.</term> <note>A reference to Luke 17:10: "So you also, when you have done everything you were told to do, should say, 'We are unworthy servants; we have only done our duty.'"</note> Rather out of pure undeserved grace / **for the sake of the merit of Christ apprehended through faith** <term>propter meritum Christi fide apprehensum</term> he becomes righteous and blessed?

->Answer: Yes.<-

6. That the preached Word / and the Holy Sacraments are **means of salvation** <term>media salutis</term>, which one should use and not despise / when they can be had / yet **God** is not bound to them / and in case of need can also **work salvation** <term>Salutem operari</term> without them / which I **illustrated to him with a similar example** <term>simili Exemplo illustriret</term>. <note>The examiner, Elias Theodor, likely provided a comparison, such as the thief on the cross, to show how God can save without formal sacraments in emergencies.</note>

->Answer: Yes.<-

7. Whether he holds the doctrine as it is taught in our churches to be right and **scriptural** <term>Schrifftmäßig</term> <note>Conforming to the Holy Scriptures.</note>.

->Answer: Yes.<-

8. Whether he also further—should **God** grant him more life and help him back to his health—intends to hold to our church and doctrine, and renounce whatever is contrary to it? And as he declared himself **affirmatively** <term>affirmativè</term> on this point, I directed and exhorted him to the **Scriptures** <term>Scripturam</term>. I urged that he should be content with **God’s** back—that is, with His revealed Word <note>original: "Gottes Rücken". This is a biblical metaphor based on Exodus 33:23; in this context, it refers to knowing God through what He has revealed in the Bible, rather than trying to see His "face" or His hidden, absolute essence.</note>. Regarding secret **revelations** <term>Revelationibus</term> and visions, it was a very uncertain business, for a man could easily imagine and dream something for himself that never happened **in actual truth** <term>in rei veritate</term>. Likewise, the devil could well cast a mist before one's eyes; but God’s Word is certain, and whoever trusts and believes in it cannot be deceived.

To this he gave the answer: He had the New Testament, which he read quite diligently. I, however, exhorted him that he should **join** <term>conjungiren</term> the Old and New [Testaments], for the Old had its regard for the New, and the New for the Old, and the New was an explanation and fulfillment of the Old. **Likewise** <term>Item</term>: He wished to refrain from writing books, given the motives I had introduced. He then told me his **occasion for writing** <term>occasionem scribendi</term> <note>The specific experiences or divine "impulses" that drove him to begin his literary career.</note>, which I leave to its own merit or lack thereof, and deem it unnecessary to recount here.

9. Whether he had also received the **Holy Supper** <note>original: "Abendmahl". Refers to Holy Communion or the Eucharist.</note> before this?

# Biography.

****** 5
<meta>Catchword: therein:</meta>

After **Master Elias Theodor** [M. El. Theod.] drafted the preceding document to be presented to the Council of that place, he first sent it to Mr. Hans Rothe for his review; the latter responded with the following.

->Reverend etc., especially beloved Sir and friend.<-

THAT you have, out of a kind will, allowed the questions which you proposed to the blessed, dear **Jacob Böhme** before Communion and subsequently committed to paper, to be communicated to me for my review via my landlady—this I acknowledge as a mark of friendship from you. And having read them through, I hereby return your manuscript <note>original: "Manuscriptum"</note> to you with gratitude.

Regarding those same questions, however: both as concerns his confession of faith <term>Confession of Faith</term> <note>original: "Glaubens-Bekäntnuß"</note> and his sacramental confession <note>original: "Beichte"</note>, you would not, in my opinion, have had any need for them. This is because **Jacob Böhme’s** confession of faith is publicly available to many in his writings—the likes of which have hardly been written since the time of the Apostles—so clear, pure, and clean, without any misunderstanding or error. Furthermore, he himself wrote excellently beautiful little treatises <note>original: "Tractätlein"</note>—not for his own sake, but at the request of many hearts hungering for heavenly wisdom—concerning the right, true, essential faith, the new birth <term>New Birth</term> <note>original: "neuen Gebuhrt," a central concept in Böhme’s theology regarding spiritual transformation</note>, Christ’s incarnation <note>original: "Menschwerdung"</note>, suffering, death, and resurrection, his two testaments, and much more besides. And

therein his beautiful and clear knowledge <term>Knowledge</term> <note>original: "Erkantnuß," referring here to a profound spiritual understanding or divine insight</note> was communicated to them. Consequently, the Sir is quite to be excused in this instance, and moreover to be praised for having had a desire to investigate the truth, and for having sought—and also found—it in such a way. And had the Sir come to him sooner, especially before his decline <note>Referring to Böhme’s final illness</note>, when he could have spoken with the Sir from a free spirit: as the Sir had good opportunity for this, and it was even (to my knowledge) offered to him, then the Sir would have listened to his answer with wonder, and would have had to perceive and recognize his high and deep divine gifts and knowledge. And especially, the Sir would also have learned whether he had also read and understood the Old Testament or not, which now his book on **Genesis** <term>Genesis</term> <note>original: "Genesin," referring to Böhme's massive work "Mysterium Magnum," a spiritual commentary on the first book of the Bible</note>, which he wrote two years ago, may be a public testimony for all the world: since it is the core

## Biography.

...of Holy Scripture, and also of all his divine and spirit-filled writings.

Regarding the secret revelations <term>Revelations</term> <note>original: "Revelationes"</note>, the good man [Böhme] is treated with too much violence and excess here, as in all other things, as if he had directed someone in such a manner as the Sir <note>Referring to the recipient, Master Elias Theodor</note> means and understands them.

But as for what the Divine Revelations <term>Revelations</term> <note>original: "Revelationes"</note> truly are (where **God’s** Spirit powerfully speaks into our soul and is the power of **God**), his books are full of them, and it is no work of fables. Rather, those who have tasted the sweetness of **God** can testify to this. And the Sir should not think at all that the blessed **Jacob Böhme** was a dreamer who allowed himself to be led and guided by the devil with his mockery and deceptive tricks in dreams and through them. No: rather all his good works (which not He, but **God** worked in him as in His instrument, and therefore were not his own, but **God’s** [within him]) were done in **God**; of which there is no need to write further here, since it is not my purpose <note>original: "Propositi"</note>.

But that the Sir also restrained him from writing books at that time and warned him against it seems somewhat strange to me, since **Christ** says:
> Let your light shine, etc. Likewise, No one sets a light under a bench, etc. Likewise, He forbids burying the talent <term>Talent</term> <note>original: "Pfundt," literally "pound," referring to the Parable of the Talents in Matthew 25:14-30</note> that **God** gives to everyone, and commands on the contrary to trade and increase it, and threatens great punishment and harm to the person who does not increase it.

Since **God** has now given him divine gifts, who was the Sir that he could resist **God**? As

## Jacob Böhme’s

Peter also confessed: for if it is from **God**, then men cannot suppress it, lest they be found to be those who wish to strive against **God** <note>This is a reference to Acts 5:39, where Gamaliel (and by extension the Apostles like Peter) argues that human efforts cannot stop a movement ordained by God.</note>. Beyond this, it was also mentioned above that he wrote nothing without an internal impulse and the heartfelt desire of several lovers of truth who guided and urged him; thus, he fulfilled the Divine Calling <term>Calling</term> <note>original: "Beruff"</note> within him.

The following questions—through which the Sir <note>Master Elias Theodor</note> wished to fathom the repentant and fervent heart and mind of the dear **Jacob Böhme**—would also be necessary, and indeed highly necessary, to be properly impressed upon every layperson, whether in general or in particular, with correct and unceasing instruction as to what true Repentance <term>Repentance</term> <note>original: "Busse"</note> actually is. This should be done in the manner and fashion found in the blessed **Jacob Böhme’s** printed booklet. If such things were earnestly and publicly urged, taught, and shown from the Pulpit <term>Pulpit</term> <note>original: "Cantzel"</note>—just as **Tauler** <note>Johannes Tauler (c. 1300–1361), a famous German mystic and preacher highly regarded by Böhme’s circle.</note> did in his time, and as **Johann Arndt** <note>Johann Arndt (1555–1621), a central figure in Lutheran pietism whose "True Christianity" emphasized inner transformation.</note> does now—there is no doubt that it would produce great benefit. However, it is not my place to issue orders regarding this; rather, I only wish to make my good intentions known to the Sir, and I leave the rest to the Sir and his supporting Ministry <term>Ministry</term> <note>original: "Ministerio," referring to the formal ecclesiastical office and its duties</note>, as is proper.

Now, since the Sir must admit that he received a correct and modest answer to all such questions of his, and therefore could and must be entirely satisfied with **Jacob Böhme** and his confession, I would now like to know all the reasons at once [for the continued opposition]...

# Biography

1. Concerning the reasons why the Master <note>Referring to Elias Theodor, the pastor tasked with Böhme's funeral</note>, despite the command of the Council, initially refused to deliver the sermon for the heir; why on the following day he sent back the **Verse** <note>original: "Dictum"; the specific biblical text chosen for the funeral sermon</note> for the funeral sermon along with the **Fee** <note>original: "Honorario"</note>; and why he also obtained an **Assurance** <term>Assurance</term> <note>original: "Assecuration"; a formal guarantee of protection or legal indemnity</note> from the Council on the second day.

2. That upon the **Pulpit** <term>Pulpit</term> <note>original: "Canzel"</note> he delivered an **Introduction** <term>Introduction</term> <note>original: "Exordium"</note> full of nothing but **Protestations** <note>original: "Protestationibus"; formal declarations or objections</note>, and specifically this **Protestation**: that he testified before the Holy Trinity that he did not share in the errors of the blessed **Jacob Böhme**. Yet, the Master must confess that he perceived no error in him, but rather agreed with him in all things. If he recognized him as a man of God, why did the Master not also publicly acknowledge him at that time, and join with him as one member to another in the Head and Body of **Christ**?

3. That the Master did not read the **Written Script** <note>original: "Præscriptum"</note> according to the **Draft** <note>original: "Concept"</note>, specifically regarding the beautiful **Music** which Böhme certainly heard—not with the external ears, but with the internal ears of his mind and spirit—within his soul four hours before his blessed departure. *Likewise*, the Master omitted the devout, heartfelt **Sighs** <note>original: "Suspiria"; breath-like prayers or aspirations</note> and prayers: first to God’s mercy, then to **Christ**, just as the thief on the cross, saying: "You kind Lord **Jesus**, take me into your kingdom." *Likewise*, that as he was about to die, he previously saw the Heavenly **Paradise** open, just as **St. Stephen** did. *Likewise*, when his soul (so to speak) departed, he cried out with a joyful mouth and with quite lovely and smiling gestures: "Oh, so I journey into **Paradise**!" And although my landlady told me the Master’s opinion...

->Jacob Böhme’s<-

...should have given some understanding of this / Yet I have not received enough of a report for me to be satisfied.

Truly, if the Master <note>Referring to the pastor, Elias Theodor</note> had read these *swan-like words and sighs* <note>original: "Cygnea verba & suspiria"; a "swan song" refers to the final words or actions of a person before death</note> before the congregation / and if they had been able to hear and listen to his blessed departure therein (which he took from this world and from us with such longing sighs and calling upon **GOD** / and his Savior) / then many pious hearts would have been moved to feel *of the dead, nothing but good* <note>original: "de mortuo nil nisi bonum"; a traditional Latin maxim suggesting one should not speak ill of the deceased</note>. However, those who are malicious **thistle-children** <note>A metaphor for spiteful or prickly people who oppose the "wheat" or the godly</note> might have thought of it whatever they wished. According to the verse / *if the interpreter is good* <note>original: "si bonus interpres"</note>. Thus, it would have been neither *unusual* <note>original: "Insolens"</note> nor new: as it happens in all funeral sermons: and specifically, I remember that in the funeral sermon for Master **Bartholomæus Stübner’s** little daughter, all her visions and similar beautiful speeches were (not unjustly) highly praised. Therefore, it is quite rightly to be noted as an *unusual thing* <note>original: "Insolens quiddam"</note> and something very significant that the Master passed over such things here *by design* <note>original: "studio"; implying the pastor intentionally omitted Böhme's final pious words to spite him</note>.

4. As also and for the 4th point: That the Master used *these unusual words and a mode unheard of until now* <note>original: "insolentia hæc verba & hactenus inauditum modum"</note> / Namely / "if he has now let his sins be a matter of heartfelt sorrow / then we will hope / that he has become a child of eternal salvation: If not, we commend him to the judgment of **GOD**": and with that, the Master concluded without the blessing which one is accustomed to wish for the deceased in their grave. In short, the entire *burial act* <note>original: "Actus Sepulturæ"</note> was full of *unusual novelties* <note>original: "insolentium novitatum"</note> / and specifically, it has never been heard of that someone did not want to bury a person <note>This refers to the pastor's initial refusal to perform the burial rites for Böhme, who was viewed as a heretic by the local church authorities</note>.

# Course of Life.

**Jacob Böhme** of blessed memory desired / that / just as he forgave all / so all should and would heartily forgive him. So let those now see to how they wish to pray correctly / and daily with their prayers / pull punishment upon their own necks: those who remained unforgiving toward him on their part / not forgiving him: And following that / first would not permit the <term>burial</term> <note>original: "sepultur"</note> / and thereafter also would not walk with him to the grave.

Law 9, Code on Heretics and Manichaeans, says **Emperor Marcian**: *It is humane and pious that even heretics be buried in legitimate graves.* <note>original: "Humanum atque pium esse hæreticos sepeliri legitimis sepulchris"</note>

Likewise Law 10 of the same Code shortly thereafter: *Heretics are to be buried in the legitimate manner like all others* <note>original: "Hæretici Legitimo modo ut cæteri sepeliuntur"</note> and in **Canon Law** <term>Jure Canonico</term> <note>The body of ecclesiastical laws used by the church</note> much more so.

If now the <term>Right of Burial</term> <note>original: "Jus Sepulturæ"</note> is granted to those / who have been convicted of a public error: How much more to those / who have only been decried as heretics out of hatred and envy / and have not been convicted of it: Indeed, who have never been summoned, heard, nor condemned, nor can they be convicted of it in all eternity / and the Lord <note>Likely referring to a local official or the sympathetic clergyman who defied the stricter faction to bury Böhme</note> has done quite rightly (although with him, as with us all / very many weaknesses have slipped in) / that he, <term>by reason of his office</term> <note>original: "Ratione Officii"</note>, attended the blessed **Jacob Böhme** much more willingly both at the <term>Communion</term> and the burial / than the others did / he shall also for this have glory, honor / and thanks before all the world / yes, before God and all pious hearts: and can all the sooner and more find peace / and powerfully comfort himself with his good conscience against the <term>slanders of the wicked</term> <note>original: "Calumnias malorum"</note>. In the meantime, however, and first and foremost, accept this my opinion *with the same spirit from which it was offered* <note>original: "ea qua profectum est mente"</note>,

It praises an official who fulfilled his duty by attending Böhme's deathbed and funeral despite opposition from others.</summary>

## Biography.

...the Master’s <note>Referring to Pastor Elias Theodor, who conducted the deathbed examination</note> intention in this question was not to bring him to a recantation <note>original: "Wider-ruff"</note>: but rather that he should confess whether in the future, if he should recover from his sickbed, he would continue to profess his adherence to them and their doctrine.

Therefore the Master is hereby kindly requested, for the sake of the truth and to untangle all misunderstanding and evil implications <term>Consequentien</term>, as well as his adversaries and slanderers <term>Calumnianten</term>. And especially for the rescue of **God’s** honor and preservation, as well as gratitude for such high and great gifts as He [God] has placed in this instrument—despised by the world—and revealed through him, so that he might not be mocked by his adversaries, nor overshadowed and darkened by the devils and their instruments.

For I do not doubt that the Master will grant my friendly and reasonable request, and because his writing—intended as a *permanent record of the event and a testimony of the truth* <note>original Latin: "Scriptum ad perpetuam rei gestæ memoriam & veritatis Testimonium"</note>—is to remain at the Town Hall, such a precedent <term>præjudicium</term> against the Master's [Böhme's] own will and opinion should not creep in by this error <term>Errore</term>, nor be allowed to harden through the passage of time <note>original Latin: "Temporis diuturnitate induriren"</note>. Furthermore, as concerns the vow owed by his hands <note>original Latin: "votum manibus debitum"; referring to the physical gesture Böhme made on his bed as a sign of his oath</note>, I will certainly believe the Master without contradiction <term>Contradiction</term> that it was in his original draft <term>Concept</term>. However, I did not hear it read aloud, nor did many others beside me, which is why I immediately spoke of it in the church with Dr. Kober. It may well be that the Master—either because of the zeal of which he seemed quite full, or because it was somewhat dark—overlooked it. Be that as it may, since the Master did not omit it intentionally <term>studio</term>, yet it occurred and was not understood by me and others...

## Mr. Abraham von Franckenberg’s Epistle, from October 21st, Anno 1641.

->May the noble Wisdom <note>original: "SopIAH." In the Böhmean tradition, Sophia represents Divine Wisdom, the feminine aspect of the Godhead and the "heavenly bride" that the seeker's soul must pursue.</note> from above gladden the hearts of those who love her, and work in them a holy will, well-pleasing to God.<-

**B**eloved in the Beloved: I have heard with particular grace that there are still not everywhere only vain enemies and despisers of the heavenly wedding—of whom there are, alas, more than too many in our already troubled fatherland—but that there are still to be found in some places loving friends, though few in number, who very diligently search after the secret wisdom. If they do not cease, they shall finally, with glorious honor, enjoy their Divine, gracious, and chaste love with overflowing joy and satisfaction of their devoted souls, in spirit and in truth.

2. To such hidden manna <term>Mannah</term> and truly Divine and Natural nectar and ambrosia <term>Nectar und Ambrosia</term> now, we have been called and invited at the start of our blessed Golden Age <note>original: "Seculo AUR (ex H) OR Æ benedictæ." This is a complex mystical cipher. It punned on *Aurora* (Böhme’s first book), the "Age of Gold" (*Aurea*), and the "Blessed Hour" (*Hora benedicta*). The "AUR" refers to Divine Light or Gold.</note> by the sounding blast of the trumpet—the victorious voice of the great **JEHOVAH**—through the one indeed rejected by men, but by God...
