## Life-Course.

...specially chosen tool and witness of **JESUS CHRIST**, <term>Jacob Böhme</term> <note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624) was a German mystic and theologian. A shoemaker by trade, he produced profound works of Christian theosophy that later influenced philosophers like Hegel and poets like Blake.</note> of Alt Seidenburg, formerly a settled inhabitant of Görlitz. Who, in his writings—which are established very high and deep above all nature—has sufficiently made known into what kind of time we have now fallen, and what further wonders and deeds of **God** are to be expected among posterity. So that we indeed have time to rise from the drunken cup of staggering of the great Babylon <note>original: "Babel." In the context of Böhmean thought, "Babylon" or "Babel" refers to the confusion of institutional religion and the corrupt state of the worldly church.</note>, and to go out to meet the calling and knocking Bridegroom.

3. Certainly this man, blessed in God, was a burning light, and perhaps he was, in a mysterious way, that New Wonder-Star in the heavens which appeared in the year 1572 (two years before the blessed man's birth) in the so-called <term>Cassiopeia</term> and <term>Milky Way</term> <note>original Latin: "via lactea."</note> or White St. James's Way <note>A traditional name for the Milky Way, often associated with the pilgrimage path to Santiago de Compostela.</note>, appearing remarkably to the European peoples as a special sign of an entirely new birth out of the upper fiery light-waters <note>In Böhme’s cosmology, "light-waters" refers to the spiritual, heavenly substance of the divine world, as opposed to the material waters of the earth.</note>, to the encouragement of many highly learned men and searching minds. Just as he then also successfully concluded his last—and as he often remarked to me in person, his most worth-reading—secret book, the <term>Great Mystery</term> <note>original Latin: "Mysterium Magnum"; this is Böhme’s massive 1623 commentary on the book of Genesis, explaining the creation of the world and the nature of evil.</note> concerning Genesis, during the years 1604 and 1607 when new stars <note>This refers to the supernovas observed by astronomers like Kepler (1604) and the shifting of the "Great Conjunctions."</note> appeared once more, at the ending of the 7th or 7th <note>The original text repeats "7th or 7th," which may be a printing error or a reference to a specific astrological sub-cycle.</note> <term>Trigonal</term> <note>Referring to the "Great Conjunctions" of Jupiter and Saturn, which cycle through the zodiacal signs in a triangular (trigonal) pattern over centuries.</note> greatest conjunction, and the beginning of the 8th among the 7 Central ones, namely in the year 1623. Whereupon he afterwards, in the year 1624, in the jubilee or 50th year of his age <note>A reference to the biblical "Jubilee," the fiftieth year which marks a time of release and return.</note>, according to the Mystery, entered again into his grave, or his magical and <term>mental principle</term> <note>original Latin-inflected German: "mentalisches Principium." In Böhmean thought, a "Principle" is a fundamental realm of reality; he refers here to Böhme returning to his spiritual origin in the mind or spirit of God.</note>.

<summary>This page describes Jacob Böhme as a divinely appointed witness and links the timing of his birth and major writings to significant celestial events, such as the supernova of 1572. It portrays his death in 1624 as a mystical return to his spiritual "principle" or origin.</summary>

# Jacob Böhme’s

4. Although it is not without cause that first the **Morning Redness Rising** <note>original: "Morgen-röhte im Auffgang." This refers to Böhme’s first major work, often known by its Latin title, *Aurora*.</note>—which is the root or mother of **Philosophy, Astrology, and Theology** etc.—as also the little book on the **Way to Christ**, which according to the order of the New Testament begins with repentance and faith for the holy entrance into the Kingdom of God, and subsequently his own person, finally even his deceased body, and at last the wretched grave and wooden cross in the churchyard itself were slandered and attacked with very ignorant zeal to the highest impropriety. Even though these matters never came to any proper hearing, nor was the blessed **Jacob Böhme** convicted of any un-Evangelical error; but rather (as can be clearly seen from the existing <term>Acts of Agony and Burial</term> <note>original Latin: "Actis agonalibus & exequialibus"; these are the formal records of his final days, confession of faith, and funeral rites.</note>, as well as from his balanced writings on **Election by Grace**, both Testaments, and the suffering, death, and resurrection of **Jesus Christ**) he was found constant and unwavering in pure and clear **Lutheranism** until his final departure. Regardless of what he elsewhere sufficiently presented in a specific <term>Apology</term> <note>A formal written defense against accusations.</note> against the evil <term>Judgment</term> <note>original Latin: "Judicium."</note> of the <term>Chief Pastor</term> <note>original Latin: "Primarii."</note> **Gregor Richter** <note>Gregor Richter (1560–1624) was the primary pastor in Görlitz and a fierce opponent of Böhme, often attacking him from the pulpit.</note> in the year 1624, which was based only on hearsay.

5. And such a dispute concerning the imitation of the Grave of Christ and His faithful witness **Jacob Böhme** right there in Görlitz is of equal <term>importance</term> as that of **Blessed Luther**, which in Wittenberg has been surrounded and besieged until now by the northern and southern peoples—to say nothing of the Eastern and Western nations—and yet (though indeed dearly purchased) into their

# Life-Course.

...not unreasonably remained under the protection and treasure of His Electoral Serenity <note>This refers to the Elector of Saxony, who provided a level of protection to Böhme toward the end of his life.</note>; so that both the faithful and precious witnesses of the past and present **Century** <note>original Latin: *Seculi*</note>—witnesses of the true, clear, and unadulterated Evangelical Light and Right—remained undisturbed in their graves by the Babylonians <note>A common metaphor in Böhme's circle for the corrupt or "captive" official church authorities</note>, just as Moses did. Thus, Evangelical Christendom would have a special mark, a sign of truth and a sign of wonder, by which it might recognize the gracious visitation of God and His Spirit in Christ JESUS, its Only and Eternal Head and Savior.

6. Therefore, regarding these **Wonder-Writings**—revealed and entrusted **directly** <note>original Latin: *immediatè*</note> by God to our **Century**—it is well to consider that they are not like the books of other men. They were not composed in the spirit of the "starry air" or from earthly, cobbled-together **Reason** <term>Vernunfft</term> <note>Böhme frequently contrasted *Vernunfft* (human intellect or logic) with *Verstand* (spiritual understanding or divine wisdom).</note>, which writes and then erases, shifting and re-arranging. Rather, they were written through the overshadowing and indwelling cooperation of the great and secret Spirit of God, from above and within, with a continuous **flow and stroke** <note>original Latin: *Ductu & Tractu*</note> of the **writing-quill**—a quill that was given to him, not taken up by his own choice. And so they were written **by his own original hand** <note>original Latin: *ipsa prima manu*</note>, of which I and others can be **eyewitnesses** <note>original Latin: *oculati testes*</note>. They were written by the **Author** himself and must be viewed and judged with eyes far different from common reason.

7. Not to mention that the "fullness of time" has not yet arrived, in which one might recognize and accept these and other hitherto hidden wonders to a satisfying degree. Such a work cannot be mastered with "unwashed hands" <note>A biblical metaphor for approaching the sacred without spiritual preparation or reverence.</note>, but must be approached in the sweat of the brow, with a holy mind and spirit sacrificed to God alone, and with a...

<summary>The author argues that Böhme's writings were directly dictated by the Holy Spirit rather than human intellect, citing eyewitness accounts of his writing process. He warns that these "Wonder-Writings" require spiritual purity to be properly understood.</summary>

# Jacob Böhme’s

will, having been started, mediated, and without looking back—yet continued and completed as if in **GOD** and through **GOD** with Christ and His Holy Spirit of Grace. Thus, the closed kingdom of **GOD** and the image of **J. C.** <note>Jesus Christ</note> sealed within it might be opened again. In those eager seekers and lovers of the glory of our **Lord** and Savior **JESUS** Christ, it may be most healthfully established with an inward and eternally joy-filled heart-shine and jubilee-sound, for the honor of the Most High and for the salvation of our senses and souls, which have been turned away from the **Divine** Light and the right path. To lose these souls is a futile thing, even if one were to gain the whole wide world; and to preserve them, we must lose ourselves and everything that is in and of us in **GOD**, the highest Good. Only then will we eternally find true, constant rest and peace in the broken-open living Rock and heart of **Jesus Christ**, Amen.

8. Toward this high and salvific goal, we should therefore on all sides—by means of Divine Grace, as much as is possible in this state of **mortality** <term>Sterbligkeit</term> <note>Böhme emphasizes the limitations of the physical, "dying" body compared to the eternal spirit.</note>—strive and labor without negligent standing-still toward the entrance of the Light. We must walk therein as long as we have it, to enjoy it here in time and thereafter in eternity.

To this end, may **Jesus Christ** help and grant us—He who is the only and true Light and salvation of our souls, who came into the world to free us from the power and night of darkness and eternal death.

# Jacob Böhme’s

...[they] guard against such an illusion regarding the gift of the Holy Spirit; indeed, they even venture to deny this gift of the Holy Spirit in these current final times, and yet they still wish to be called clergymen. 
It has not been so long since Jacob Böhme lived, and his person was well enough known here. I, however, did not know him, because at the time when his book-writing first became public, I was still somewhat young; and when, after several years, he again took up the writing which he had ceased upon a prohibition, and was thereby denounced as a heretic from the pulpit by the <term>Head Pastor</term> <note>original: Primario Pastore; referring to Gregor Richter, the lead pastor in Görlitz and Böhme's primary antagonist.</note>, I was mostly absent. 

But in the year 1624, shortly after his death, I became acquainted with several of his most prominent friends and admirers, who had associated with him for a long and frequent time: This occurred when Mr. Jonas Liebing, the judge at that time in Weissende, located four miles from Nuremberg (with whom I had stayed shortly before), gave me the occasion for this by writing to me twice, requesting that, as a favor to him and Mr. Christian Beckmann, Rector of the Gymnasium at Amberg, I would provide a specific report concerning Jacob Böhme. This was because they also could not easily believe that an <term>idiot</term> <note>original: Idiot. In the 17th century, this term was used in its classical sense to mean a "layman" or a "private person" without formal university education or professional status, rather than a lack of intelligence.</note> should possess such a deep and uncommon knowledge of God and Nature. 

Beckmann's words in his letter, directed among others to Liebing, are these: *“Last winter a friend wrote to me <note>original Latin: Superiori hyeme scripserat ad me amicus</note>: that in Görlitz there is a common man <note>original: virum plebejum</note> and otherwise <term>unlearned</term> <note>original Greek: αμαθη (amathē); meaning illiterate or without formal education</note>, Jacob Böhme by name, who, anointed by a unique grace of the spirit, both speaks various languages, and moreover [writes] books of much...”*

<summary>A contemporary account from 1624 detailing how even learned scholars like Christian Beckmann were skeptical that an uneducated "idiot" like Böhme could possess such profound spiritual and natural knowledge. The narrator describes Böhme’s persecution by the local head pastor and the growing interest in his writings following his death.</summary>

# Life-Path

<sig>****** 2</sig>

...completes [works] full of wisdom. From that time, I did not cease to inquire diligently whether it were so, etc. At last, a few days ago, I came to Eger <note>original: Egram; the German name for the city of Cheb in the modern-day Czech Republic.</note>, and there among other things I saw in the house of a friend three manuscript books—and indeed quite large ones—by that Bohemian. What shall I say? As I read, how I was amazed! Is it possible for a man from the masses, not educated in the Schools, to approach such profound Mysteries and to write so elegantly! Truly, the method itself and the exposition of hidden things make me doubt the Author. Böhme is said to be an <term>idiot</term> <note>original: "idiota"; in the 17th century, this referred to a "layman" or a person without formal academic training in Latin and theology, rather than its modern derogatory meaning.</note>.
<margin>}</margin>
I cannot yet believe it, unless I am made more certain that such a man exists in Görlitz and has written such things, and this by the testimony of one or two more sincere men, etc. Among the aforementioned former friends of **Jacob Böhme**, there was one in particular whose company I often and greatly enjoyed, who was able to recount how a **Doctor of Medicine** here named **Tobias Rober**, whom I still knew well, had several times put **Jacob Böhme** to the test regarding his knowledge of the <term>Natural Language</term> <note>original: Natursprache; the mystical belief that names should reveal the inner essence of things, a language lost since the Fall of Man.</note>. In this, as close friends while walking together, they showed one another the flowers, herbs, and other growths of the earth, and **Jacob Böhme**, from their external **Signature** and form, immediately indicated the internal power, effect, and quality, using the letters, syllables, and words of the name spoken and given to them. However, he preferred to hear the names in the Hebrew language above others, as it was closest to the **Natural Language**. And when those names were not known, he asked for the Greek ones. And whenever the **Physician** intentionally gave a wrong name, the other [Böhme], when comparing its quality against the plant and its **Signature**, such as the **Form**...

I will say nothing of those who were also known to me, who through this man’s presence and penetrating spiritual discourse underwent a remarkable and rapid change of heart and renewal of life: so much so, that whereas they were previously entirely devoted to the vanity of this world and the lusts of the flesh, and behaved toward their subjects no differently than ravening wolves, afterward, to everyone’s astonishment, they became enemies of all excess and dealt with them like patient sheep, mourning their former scandalous lives with constant repentance. From none of those who knew him have I ever heard, nor could I learn through others, that he should not be accepted as the one who wrote the books that have since appeared under the name *Teutonici* <note>original: "Teutonici" (Latin); Böhme was widely known as the "Philosophus Teutonicus" or the "Teutonic Philosopher."</note> in High- and Low-German, as well as English- and partly Latin languages. # Jacob Böhme’s

...had the spirit of examination, and to whom he, because of his high gifts, was able to give sufficient account, speech, and answer, as well as provide an abundant explanation of his books. This he also did, and thus bore witness—verbally and in person—to what he had written, with great power and miraculous effect upon his listeners. When he stayed in Dresden for a considerable time with the most distinguished Electoral Privy Councilor, and also wrote there, he had to undergo an <term>examination</term> <note>original: "Examen"; a formal inquiry into his theological views by the religious authorities in Dresden.</note> due to that opportunity. No one but he could have spoken then. I have certain news of this from that same place, dated December 2nd, 1661, in these words: "I have inquired as to what should be held regarding Jacob Böhme’s <term>examination</term> <note>original: "Examination."</note> in Dresden, and [found] that it certainly took place."

He was placed in custody for it, and his written book in <term>quarto folio</term> <note>original: "4to fol."; a size format for a manuscript, referring here to his first work, "Aurora."</note> was immediately fetched from his house by the city servant. Following this, he was released from prison and warned to desist from such matters.

Likewise, on Tuesday, <note>The symbol ♂ (Mars) denotes Tuesday.</note> July 30th, Jacob Böhme the shoemaker was summoned by the <term>preachers</term> <note>original: "Prædicanten"; the local Lutheran clergy.</note> to the residence of the <term>lead pastor</term> <note>original: "Primarii"; refers to the head of the local church hierarchy.</note> and seriously interrogated regarding his <term>confession</term> <note>original: "Confession"; his formal statement of faith.</note>. # Course of Life

[Pastor] Richter delivered a sharp sermon against the shoemaker <term>J. B.</term> <note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), the mystic philosopher and shoemaker.</note>

So it stands, my beloved friend, that at that time, no one else but the shoemaker was accepted and held here as the so-called <term>enthusiast</term> <note>original: "Enthusiasten"; in the 17th century, this was a derogatory label for someone believed to be experiencing religious delusions or claiming direct divine inspiration outside of church dogma.</note> and as the author of the written book (which was *Aurora*, and which, as I have since learned from foreign places, was taken from our town hall to a certain location in <term>Dresden</term>). In the same way, a former mayor of this city, <term>Johann Emerich</term> <note>Johann Emerich (1559–1626) was a wealthy mayor of Görlitz; his family was responsible for building the city's famous replica of the Holy Sepulchre.</note> (whose family ancestors built the Holy Sepulchre), testifies in his diary, where in the year 1624 he recorded these words:

> On November 7/17, the shoemaker died, whom <term>Gregorius Richter</term> <note>Gregorius Richter (1560–1624) was the chief pastor of Görlitz and Böhme’s primary antagonist, who viewed Böhme’s writings as heretical.</note> frequently and greatly slandered; however, the shoemaker sufficiently defended himself, etc. It would have been better if the <term>Head Pastor</term> <note>original: "Primarius"; the title for the lead minister of the city’s main church.</note> had left the shoemaker in peace; he gained little honor from it, etc. It would certainly have been much better, for that good man, the shoemaker—of whom I never heard anything unseemly—would not have needed to write an <term>apology</term> <note>original: "Apologiam"; a formal written defense of his beliefs and character.</note> against the pastor's slanderous papers to save his honest name and reveal the other's own shame. But the Head Pastor, through the means of the world's slanderous tongues, was destined to make him known and, to his own disadvantage, promote Böhme's honor among impartial minds.

In short, during the lifetime of <term>Jacob Böhme</term>, I believe there was no one here who did not consider him the right and only author, or the true instrument of the writings in question. It was only after his death that the subsequent "new world," and those who did not know him, especially in the...

# Jacob Böhme’s

Outsiders who wish to cast doubt upon the high knowledge of such a layman and humble man.

A short biography <note>original: "Lebens-Lauff"; literally a "life-run," the standard 17th-century term for a biography.</note> alongside a register of the books of **Jacob Böhme** was composed in Latin in the year **1637** by the well-regarded nobleman **Abraham von Franckenberg** <note>Franckenberg (1593–1652) was a close friend and the primary biographer of Böhme; he was a mystic and nobleman from Silesia.</note> and given to a good friend. This friend brought it to **Amsterdam** in the following year, **1638**, and translated it into High German for the sake of a devotee. Since that time, once translated into German, it has been included with the published books. To be sure, this was done without the author's <insert>Franckenberg's</insert> prior knowledge; had he anticipated such a possibility, he would have presented the matter according to the nature of the German language, not only much more clearly, but also more extensively and with more detail—or perhaps he would have kept those divine mysteries, which resided within the person <insert>Böhme</insert> and were made known to him above others, entirely hidden from a mocking world. The works, which testify to this secret man and friend of God—namely his books, now known far and wide—are already sufficiently slandered by those who despise them.

In the year **1639**, **Abraham Wilhelmsoon van Beyerland** <note>A wealthy Dutch merchant and collector who dedicated his life and fortune to preserving and publishing Böhme’s manuscripts.</note>, a citizen and merchant of **Amsterdam**, first translated these books into his mother tongue—or Dutch <note>original: "Niederteutsche Sprache," literally "Low German," which in this period often referred to Dutch in a broad linguistic sense.</note>—with special effort and great diligence, and had them printed at his own considerable expense. Most of these writings I had already seen and held in my hands here in **Görlitz** in the years **1624** and **1625** and the following years, before they had even arrived in **Holland**. The author's own handwriting <note>The writer is referring to Böhme’s original autograph manuscripts.</note> of most, or at least the most prominent, of these writings was, about <margin>catchword: 11 years</margin>...

# Biography <note>original: "Lebens-Lauff"; literally a "life-run," the standard 17th-century term for a biography.</note>
****** 5

11 years ago, just as I had returned home from my twenty-five-year absence, I found [these writings] among the estate of one of my good old friends after his death. However, his son <note>The German "Vatter" here likely refers to the "father of the estate" or is a misprint/dialect usage for the heir/son, given he is described as a "young man."</note>—a young man—gave these to someone else here, and he in turn gave them for about 3 or 4 thalers (which he never actually received) to a merchant in Lauben <note>Lubań, a town in Lower Silesia.</note>. This merchant is now said to be offering them for sale here and there, wanting to sell them for 100 ducats, and for that reason, he has them stored in Leipzig. I have indeed made arrangements to see if they might be rescued from unworthy hands and preserved from destruction, but it is not easy to obtain anything for nothing from a greedy man.

If I had known of such a treasure being held by the person mentioned—whom I visited frequently at his warehouse—I have no doubt I would have come to share in it. The good man, perhaps because he held a public office <note>original: "publico Officio"</note>, might have been afraid, or at least did not expect death to come so soon. Otherwise, in Liegnitz <note>Legnica, Poland.</note>, one can find in [Böhme's] own hand—as I have been informed—the book of the *Forty Questions on the Soul* <note>original: "40. Fragen von der Seelen"</note>, as well as several epistles scattered here and there. Furthermore, there is a copy of the *Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterii Magni"</note> there, almost a quarter of which was corrected by the author's <note>original: "Authoris"</note> own hand. Something else in that same hand might likely be found among the heirs of the previously mentioned Beyerland, who otherwise acquired various copies of [Böhme's] books for a great deal of money in order to compare and <term>collate</term> <note>The process of comparing different manuscripts of the same work to identify variations and correct errors.</note> them when necessary, thus repairing any discovered defects. The author's own handwriting, whether there are 1, 2, or 3 sheets, or perhaps a day's work <note>original: "Tag-werck"; the amount of text written in a single day.</note> available...

# Jacob Böhme’s

[...having] been <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's description of Böhme's manuscripts.</note>, two nearby landowners—who were brothers—immediately had them collected, copied, and then sent them further to others who did the same: so that the first copies are likely the best, and are to be found among Beyerland’s estate <note>Abraham von Beyerland (1587–1648) was a wealthy Dutch merchant who dedicated his life and fortune to collecting and publishing Böhme’s works in Amsterdam.</note>.

Of Jacob Böhme’s sons, however, none are still living. The book on the *Last Judgment* <note>original: "Jüngsten Gerichte"</note> is said to no longer exist, but rather to have been destroyed in the fire at Groß-Glogau <note>Głogów, a city in Lower Silesia that suffered significant damage during the Thirty Years' War.</note>. The [work] concerning the *Last Times* <note>original: "letzten Zeiten"</note>, which I do not possess, I intend to seek out in Silesia: although it is hoped, according to reports received, that all the works of Jacob Böhme in the High German language <term>High German: "Hochdeutsche Sprache," referring to the standardized literary German used in the manuscripts to reach a wider audience beyond local dialects.</term> shall shortly be published together and as a whole. With this, I commend the same to God’s gracious protection.

-> Of my especially favorable Lord <-
-> and worthy friend <-

Görlitz,
February 21,
1669.

Your willing servant and friend,
E. H. <note>These initials likely refer to Erasmus Hinner, a close associate of Böhme’s circle in Görlitz.</note>

# Conclusion.

**F**rom all of the preceding, the favorable reader has understood in detail and sufficiently (as much as has been known to us through writings until now) who this **Author**, the godly **Jacob Böhme**, was; his life’s journey, and his heavy persecution for the sake of the exceedingly high Divine knowledge and revelation—not only during his life, but even unto the grave. Thus, for his own part and person, he abundantly fulfilled the remaining sufferings of **Jesus Christ** <note>This is a reference to Colossians 1:24, where the Apostle Paul speaks of "filling up what is lacking in Christ’s afflictions" through his own trials.</note> as a chosen instrument and faithful member, and has left to us in his holy writings such a piercing voice of love and earnestness for our constant encouragement and imitation. For this pearl and precious jewel <note>Refers to the "Pearl of Great Price" from Matthew 13:45–46, a common metaphor Böhme used for spiritual enlightenment.</note> (if it is otherwise received in the heart and mind for fruitfulness), the Most High can never be thanked enough, as those testify who have attained a tiny spark of his knowledge <term>Knowledge: "Erkantnuß" in Böhme’s vocabulary refers not to mere intellectual data, but to a direct, experiential spiritual realization or "gnosis."</term>, and who struggle for it ever more and more, and will testify further to those who come after.

However, it is well-intentioned to remember this for the prevention of great danger to the soul and other ruinous disorder and offense: that just as at all times, according to the common proverb, where **God** has built his church, the devil has set his chapel beside it; so particularly at this time, one must watch out and pray for the spirit of testing according to the mind of Christ and his truth. This spirit leads the soul in deep humility and love toward Divine union in the spirit and toward member-like, brotherly love—as not only this **Author**, but all holy teachers, prophets, and apostles have done—so that one does not [turn away] from the single goal and corner-

The blessed author <note>Referring to the mystic Jacob Böhme (1575–1624).</note> thoroughly, from the heart, and extensively revealed this in an **Apology** <note>A formal defense of his ideas, likely his "Apologia against Balthasar Tilke."</note> and left it as a guideline for descendants. Moreover, to counter and prevent the secret deceit of the wicked enemy and the self-corrupted and seductive reason, he wrote that precious little book called *Of True Yieldedness* <term>Yieldedness</term> <note>original: "Gelassenheit" — a central concept in German mysticism meaning the release of one’s own will and ego to God.</note>. **H**owever, among other things, a concerning point has occurred to me, about which I would like Your Affection’s <note>original: "E. L." (Euer Liebden) — a formal and affectionate honorific.</note> explanation and meaning, so that I might be edified by it. This concerns the person of the Angel with the <term>Eternal Gospel</term> <note>original: "ewigen Evangelio" — a reference to Revelation 14:6. In 17th-century mysticism, this often symbolized a final, spiritual stage of Christianity.</note>, whom I see Your Affection applying to yourself, as if you alone wish to be or represent that figure before others.

Now, it is indeed true that **God** the Most High—according to the manner and time of His various revelations—has gifted and sent out His faithful messengers and witnesses, the holy Prophets and Apostles, with the active power of His Spirit and Word. They were sent to publicly announce to the people their transgression and the punishment belonging to it, as well as the comfort and protection of grace following true repentance and conversion. They were to represent this effectively and tangibly through accompanying signs, which they carried out with complete obedience and total self-denial even unto death, with all patience and humility. Of this, the books of Holy Divine Scripture give us sufficient testimony.

But it is not expressly found in those same scriptures that one or another among the servants of **God** ever held or presented himself as an Angel of **God** or as something special. Much less did they force themselves upon others with power or compel people to follow them, as one nevertheless sees happening almost everywhere today by some newly-emerged spirits and prophets. One calls himself David, another Elijah, one Michael, another—

As far as the writings of this blessed author, **I.B.** <note>I.B. refers to Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), the influential German Christian mystic whose work "Aurora" caused a great stir in religious circles.</note>, are concerned, they are now no longer unknown, although they are still not without resistance from the Devil and his instruments. For just as these writings are a precious birth inwardly in **GOD**, and must also be recognized from that source, so too has the hand of the Most High powerfully protected them externally; to the mockery of the Destroyer, He has pulled them from the Destroyer's jaws and preserved them from destruction.

For shortly after the death of the blessed author, when one of these writings, through the providence of **God**, came to **Amsterdam** and into the hands of a pious and simple merchant named **Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt**, he was immediately ignited by it. From then on, he did not cease to strive after all the rest of the works, especially as he sought out the then still-living close friends of the blessed **Jacob Böhme**, some of whom were noble gen<insert>tlemen</insert>... <margin>The preservation of the manuscripts in Amsterdam</margin>

<summary>This page defends Jacob Böhme against accusations of heresy and "fanaticism," characterizing his writings as divinely protected. It also credits the Dutch merchant Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt with beginning the effort to collect and publish Böhme's scattered manuscripts.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt, Amsterdam, mysticism, religious persecution, Enthusiasts, Schwärmer, spiritual baptism, manuscript preservation</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt’s efforts to collect the scattered writings of the mystic Jacob Böhme. The previous page ended with a warning to those who mocked Böhme, contrasting their intellectual pride with the humble spiritual foundation needed to understand his work. This section details the providential survival of the manuscripts during their transport across war-torn Germany.</meta>

## Conclusion.

[He] sought out learned people, such as Doctors, and maintained written correspondence with them for the sake of these books; he spared no expense to purchase them wherever they could be found—in which **GOD** also granted him success. For while they were otherwise scattered individually here and there among admirers—highly valued and even kept secret—most of them were kept together by the late brothers, Mr. Carl and Michael von Endern. These men were the most esteemed friends and patrons of the blessed **I. B.** <note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624)</note>, to whom he first entrusted himself and through whom he became known. These manuscripts were not the originals, but rather the first and most accurate copies, which had been proofread by Mr. Carl von Endern. 

These same copies had come to Mr. Hans Rothe in Görlitz, from whom he [Van Beyerlandt] later purchased them for 100 <term>Reichsthaler</term> <note>A standard silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire; 100 Reichsthaler was a significant sum, equivalent to several months' wages for a skilled craftsman.</note>. He arranged for them to be delivered to his correspondent in Leipzig, and subsequently ordered that person to send them to his correspondent in Hamburg whenever the opportunity arose. 

When, at one point, a convoy of twenty freight wagons loaded with merchant goods set out from Leipzig for Hamburg, the small chest containing the aforementioned writings was loaded onto one of them. While they were en route, a misfortune occurred: they encountered a troop of marauding cavalry—as there was not yet peace in Germany <note>This refers to the Thirty Years' War (1618–1648), a period of extreme instability and violence where travel was highly dangerous due to mercenary groups.</note>. All the other wagons were completely plundered of their merchant goods, except for this one, which they left behind. Thus, under the protection of **GOD**, it escaped the danger and was delivered to Hamburg. Before it arrived there, however, the correspondent in Leipzig had already received news that the wagons had been plundered; believing the aforementioned items had perished along with the rest, he wrote...

<summary>This page recounts how Abraham van Beyerlandt acquired Jacob Böhme's manuscripts from the von Endern brothers and Hans Rothe, and describes the miraculous survival of these papers when a merchant convoy was looted by cavalry during the Thirty Years' War.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt, Carl von Endern, Michael von Endern, Hans Rothe, manuscripts, Thirty Years' War, Görlitz, Leipzig, Hamburg, providence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt’s quest to recover the manuscripts of Jacob Böhme. # Conclusion.

...therefore, a letter was sent via post to Beyerlandt in Amsterdam. Meanwhile, while the letter from Leipzig was traveling to Amsterdam, the small chest of books in Hamburg was placed on a ship and also sent to Amsterdam. It arrived in Amsterdam with a fair wind on the very same day that the other letter arrived. 

When the late Beyerlandt first received the letter from Leipzig and learned of the misfortune <note>Presumably the plundering of the wagon convoy mentioned on the previous page.</note>, he became very distressed and sad—not so much for the sake of the money, but because of the loss of the hoped-for treasure. Accordingly, around noon, he went to the Exchange <note>The Amsterdam Stock Exchange, the center of world trade at the time, where Beyerlandt worked as a merchant.</note> to conduct his business. Shortly thereafter, the skipper from Hamburg arrived with the chest of books and delivered it to his wife. Without asking what was inside, she had it set down in the entryway. 

An hour or two later, when he returned home and saw an unfamiliar chest, he asked his wife what it was. When she told him it had come from Hamburg, he opened both the letter and the chest. He found that **GOD** miraculously protects His Word, had fulfilled his wish, and overwhelmed him with particularly great joy.

Yet he did not find himself satisfied with these [copies] alone, but for the sake of greater certainty, he sought after the author's original manuscripts themselves. However, these were more difficult to obtain. For example, the great work on Genesis, <term>The Great Mystery</term> <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum." This is one of Böhme's most significant works, an expansive spiritual commentary on the first book of the Bible.</note>, had been specifically dedicated and left in the original by the late author to the aforementioned Mr. Abraham von Franckenberg <note>Abraham von Franckenberg (1593–1652) was a German mystic and Böhme’s primary biographer and disciple.</note>—a particular lover of the secrets of God in the Scriptures. This manuscript, as well as the one on the *Six Points*, had been stolen by soldiers during the wartime turmoils of that era <note>The Thirty Years' War, which saw the frequent looting of libraries and private estates.</note> along with other fine writings, and taken to Dresden. Nevertheless, **GOD** the Lord fulfilled his desire in this matter to some extent as well...

<summary>This page describes the "miraculous" delivery of Böhme's manuscripts to Amsterdam despite wartime disruptions and details Beyerlandt's ongoing efforts to track down the author's original handwritten documents.</summary>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt and his efforts to collect, translate, and publish the works of Jacob Böhme. It follows the account of him receiving a miraculous shipment of manuscripts in Amsterdam during the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

# Conclusion.

...that he received the *Aurora* <note>original: "Morgenröhte"; Böhme's first work, meaning "The Day-Spring," written in 1612.</note> as the author's first book in the original manuscript from Mr. Georg Pflug, Court Marshal to the Elector of Saxony, as mentioned above in the Biography, section 13. After that time, he also obtained from elsewhere: 2. *Of the Sacraments of Christ*. 3. *Of Resignation*. 4. *The Dialogue between the Enlightened and Unenlightened Soul*. 5. The *Apology* against the libelous card of the Head Pastor Richter <note>Gregory Richter, the primary ecclesiastical opponent of Böhme in Görlitz.</note>. 6. An incomplete fragment of an *Apology* against Balthasar Tilke. 7. Approximately 16 pieces of Epistles <note>Letters</note>, a portion of which Mr. [Abraham von] Franckenberg, as well as other devotees, shared with him. But as mentioned, in order to free himself from doubt as to whether something had been omitted here or there during copying, he also sought after other good and correct transcripts and purchased them, so that he gathered together 3 or 4 copies of almost every treatise.

Thereupon, he gratefully wished to "trade with his talents" <note>an allusion to the Parable of the Talents in Matthew 25:14-30</note> for the honor of **GOD** and for the love of his neighbor. He first set himself to reviewing them one after another in his mother tongue <note>Dutch</note>, so that his Dutch nation might also share in this High German gift of grace—especially because, out of pride, the world often despises and tramples underfoot the lowly instrument <note>A reference to Böhme's humble status as a shoemaker.</note>. Sparing no expense, he promoted the publication of most of them, one by one, in the most beautiful and clean manner, and provided the Netherlands with them—except for the *Aurora* and the *Signatura Rerum* <note>original: "The Signature of All Things."</note>, which never appeared in Dutch <note>original: "Niederteusch."</note>, as well as the *Mysterium Magnum* <note>original: "The Great Mystery."</note>, which he brought to light in High German in Quarto format due to the persistent urging of the devotees.

Thus, this merchant, wise in **God**, sought and found the hidden pearl in the field <note>A reference to the Parable of the Hidden Treasure in Matthew 13:44.</note>, and fruitfully...

<summary>This page details how Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt systematically collected multiple copies of Jacob Böhme’s manuscripts to ensure accuracy and then translated and published them in Dutch at his own expense.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt, Jacob Böhme, Aurora, Georg Pflug, translation, Amsterdam, publishing history, Mysterium Magnum, Abraham von Franckenberg</keywords>

The catchword "groß" from the previous page likely referred to the "great danger" mentioned at the start of this page.</meta>

# Conclusion.

...truly utilized and increased <note>This continues the biblical metaphor of the "Parable of the Talents," where a servant is expected to grow the resources entrusted to him by his master.</note>; although in the **publication** of these writings he also had to endure not a few attacks from adversaries with great danger, yet the hellish bird of prey <note>Likely a metaphor for the devil or the religious censors who sought to suppress "heretical" mystical writings.</note> was not able to take the blessing from him, which **GOD** increased for him through this, and set aside for him until the resurrection of the faithful. He died in the year 1648, and left the mentioned writings to his eldest son (who told me this history himself orally) with a faithful recommendation to preserve them well.

But since he also died some time ago, they have been preserved by his heirs until now, from whom we have now purchased them all in order to prepare this **edition** from them. One would hardly believe what gross defects we found in the previous edition, committed through careless negligence, not to mention the toil and labor we had with the <term>collating</term> <note>The process of comparing different manuscript copies of the same text to identify errors and determine the most authentic version.</note>; all of which was nevertheless performed with all willingness out of love for this divine gift to propagate His word, and to perform a faithful service to the **German Nation**, if perhaps she might open her eyes, and would again gratefully accept this unexpected sign of grace from God with proper acknowledgment of repentance—that which she once, to the shame and ruin of her irrationality, despised and threw away, according to the prophecy of the blessed **author** <note>original: "Authoris"; referring to Jacob Böhme.</note> in his 50th **Epistle** <note>Letter</note>, verse 10:
> What my **fatherland** throws 
> away, that foreign peoples will pick 
> up with joy.

Thus a new, clean, and correct **edition** is hereby presented to the kindhearted reader, which he, in consideration of the divine gift, as well as our unspared...

<summary>This page concludes the history of the Böhme manuscripts, detailing how they passed from Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt to his son and eventually to the current publishers, who corrected many errors found in previous versions. It frames the publication as a fulfillment of Böhme's prophecy that his work would be rejected by his own country but embraced by others.</summary>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface or publishing note for the works of Jacob Böhme, following the account of Abraham Willemsz. van Beyerlandt’s efforts to preserve the manuscripts. It explains the editorial decisions regarding the order of the texts, the physical format of the book, and the correction of printing errors.</meta>

# Conclusion.

...accept with due recognition the great expense and applied diligence in gratitude toward the Highest for such Light, and use it for the edification of his salvation in Divine wisdom.

The order of the books, as the Author wrote them one after another, is recorded previously in his **Biography** §. 16; and no particular change has been made to this, because each book begins and ends with its own page numbering, except in *The Way to Christ*, where nine of the smallest treatises were collected under a continuous page numbering; there, the *Supersensual Life* <note>original: "übersinnlichen Leben"</note>, which deals with *Divine Contemplation* <note>original: "Göttlicher Beschauligkeit"</note> as the same subject matter, and the *Conversation of the Enlightened Soul*, the *Four Complexions* <note>Böhme’s work on the four temperaments: choleric, sanguine, phlegmatic, and melancholic.</note> were also added; likewise, the three kinds of **Apologies** <note>Defenses written by Böhme against his theological critics.</note> to Stiefel, Tilken, and Richter <note>Balthasar Tilke, Esaias Stiefel, and Gregorius Richter were contemporary critics or rivals of Böhme’s mystical theology.</note> were grouped together.

We have specifically chosen and preferred the size of the <term>format</term> <note>The physical dimensions of the book.</note> this time for the sake of convenience and other considerations; and because most [of the treatises] are of small scope, each person can have them bound individually according to their preference, or several together, whether according to their chronological order or regardless of that, according to their own pleasure.

If, however, despite all close supervision in the <term>correcting</term> <note>Proofreading during the printing process.</note>, some printing errors were overlooked—as can easily happen in such a tedious work of constant proofreading—the favorable reader will know how to overlook them, and correct them wherever they are added behind each explanation of the <term>copper-plate</term> <note>original: "Kupfer-blättleins"; referring to the engraved illustrations common in Böhme’s editions, which often contained emblematic meanings.</note>. For the rest, may you be faithfully commended to Divine Love in Christ JESUS, to whom be thanks, honor, praise, and glory in Majesty for such revelation and all grace and benefit.

->E N D.<-

### Böhme, Jakob, 1575-1624 / Frankenberg, Abraham von, 1593-1652 <note>Abraham von Frankenberg was a close friend and the first biographer of Böhme; he was instrumental in preserving and spreading Böhme’s mystical teachings.</note>

All the **Theosophical Works** <note>original: "Theosophische Wercken." Theosophy here refers to "divine wisdom," a tradition of seeking direct knowledge of the nature of God and the universe.</note> of the Blessed and Highly-Enlightened **Jacob Böhme**, the **Teutonic Philosopher** <note>original: "Teutonici Philosophi." A Latin title often given to Böhme to emphasize his role as a philosopher writing in the German (Teutonic) tongue rather than scholarly Latin.</note>; wherein all the deep mysteries of God, of eternal and temporal Nature and Creation, along with the true foundation of the Christian Religion and of Godliness, are revealed according to the Apostolic testimony. Corrected with the greatest diligence, partly from the Author's own originals and partly from the first and most carefully revised copies.

Volume: [1]

Amsterdam (1682)
